# Individual and State — Complete Learning Session

> **Syllabus, verbatim:** *Individual and State : Rights; Duties and Accountability.* — UPSC Philosophy Paper II, Section A, item 3. 📝 The printed paper carries a space before the colon; it is preserved only inside a verbatim quotation of the syllabus. Three terms are printed and only three: **rights**, **duties** and **accountability**. Locke, Bentham, Hohfeld, Gandhi, Hobbes, Rousseau, Plato, Aristotle, Hegel, Green, Dworkin, Raz and Simmons enter because owned previous-year parts cannot be answered without them; Austin's command theory and the legal finality of sovereignty, the full theory of anarchism and Marxism as ideologies, monarchy, theocracy and democracy as forms, the equality–justice–liberty triad itself, recognition and group rights, gendered private power and the theories of punishment belong to later owners and are routed, not developed.
> **Evidence discipline:** ✅ marks doctrine on which the standard sources converge, ⚠️ marks analytical reconstruction for exam use, and ❓ marks a live scholarly dispute or a contested attribution. Model answers in this package are independent learner practice and are **never** official UPSC keys: UPSC publishes no descriptive model answer for Philosophy Paper II. Works are cited by author, title and — where the repository verifies it — year only: **Dworkin, *Taking Rights Seriously*, 1977**; **Thoreau, *Civil Disobedience*, 1849, first published as "Resistance to Civil Government"**; **the Salt Satyagraha of 1930, the march begun in March 1930 and the salt law broken on 6 April 1930**; **Ambedkar's concluding address to the Constituent Assembly, 25 November 1949**; **the Constitution (Forty-second Amendment) Act, 1976, which inserted Article 51A**. No page, chapter, edition or verbatim wording is asserted for Locke, Hobbes, Rousseau, Bentham, Hohfeld, Hart, Rawls, Simmons, Raz, Dworkin, Thoreau, Gandhi or Ambedkar, and the only quoted phrases are the three-word phrases the sources themselves fix — Bentham's "nonsense upon stilts" and Ambedkar's "grammar of anarchy".
> **Terminology rule:** the English concept leads and the technical term follows immediately in parentheses — *a justified claim, liberty, power or immunity recognised within a moral or legal order (a right)*, *a claim that others owe conduct (claim-right)*, *a freedom to act that imposes no positive duty (liberty or privilege)*, *an ability to alter legal relations (power)*, *a protection against alteration by others (immunity)*, *the pairing of a right with the duty it entails (correlativity)*, *rights held prior to and independent of the state (natural rights)*, *rights created by an enforceable legal order (legal rights)*, *rights justified by ethical reasoning before legal recognition (moral rights)*, *rights as normative control over another's duty (will theory)*, *rights as protections of significant interests (interest theory)*, *an obligation owed to others, community or institutions (a duty)*, *the duty-centred moral order of the Indian tradition (dharma)*, *ethical self-rule as well as political independence (swaraj)*, *truth-force, open self-suffering resistance (satyāgraha)*, *answerability, enforceability and responsiveness for the exercise of public power (accountability)*, *the state as an instrument created by prior individuals (mechanistic or artificial theory)*, *the political whole as partly constitutive of the person's good (organic or ethical theory)*, *a protected sphere of non-interference (negative freedom)*, *effective capacity to pursue a life-plan (positive freedom)*, *coercion of a competent adult only to prevent harm to others (the harm principle)*, *interference with liberty for the person's own good (paternalism)*, *the state securing fair terms without imposing one ideal of the good (neutrality)*, *the state promoting genuine goods or virtues (perfectionism)*, *a moral duty to obey the law because it is the law (political obligation)*, *a reason to obey independent of the law's content (content-independence)*, *taking a cooperative benefit while refusing its burden (free-riding)*, *a directive that replaces rather than merely outweighs private calculation (pre-emptive reason)*, *authority justified because it improves conformity with reasons that already apply (the service conception)*, *standards of justice with weight rather than all-or-nothing application (principles)*, *refusal to be personally complicit in wrong (conscientious objection)*, *public, non-violent, penalty-accepting breach of a specific law (civil disobedience)*, *obstruction of a grave particular injustice (resistance)*, *replacement of the constitutional order itself (revolution)*. A technical term may sharpen an English explanation; it may never replace one.

## BASIC LEARNING SESSION

### CONCEPT SPINE — ENGLISH FIRST, TECHNICAL TERM IMMEDIATELY AFTER

*Caption: the whole clause on one screen. One relation read in three directions, one prior question the printed terms presuppose, six pictures of what the state is, and the three-way thesis that closes almost every answer in this cluster.*

### ANSWER-WORTHINESS — WHAT EARNS MARKS AND WHAT DOES NOT

| Move | Earns marks | Loses marks |
|---|---|---|
| Opening the answer | Naming which **direction** of the relation the stem tests — claim against the state, obligation through social life, or answerability of power — inside the first three lines | "Man is a social animal and the state is the highest institution since ancient times…" |
| Defining a right | The analytic vocabulary: *claim-right*, *liberty or privilege*, *power*, *immunity*, and the justification standard being used | "A right is something every human being deserves by birth" |
| Handling Bentham | He rejects **natural** rights as "nonsense upon stilts", not rights as such; legal rights need claimant, duty-bearer and remedy | Writing that Bentham "denied all rights", which makes the 2025 correlativity question unanswerable |
| Handling correlativity | Splitting the question: **claim-rights** have strict correlative duties, **liberties** do not — so the connection is conceptual for one class and institutional for the rest | "Rights and duties are two sides of the same coin" |
| Handling Gandhi | Duty as the **moral source** of rights, tied to conscience, non-violence and mutuality — with Ambedkar's objection stated, not suppressed | Presenting Gandhi as anti-rights, or as a straightforward traditionalist |
| Handling accountability | The three dimensions — answerability, enforceability, responsiveness — and the three arenas — political, legal, moral | Reducing accountability to elections, or to punishment |
| Handling the contract | What each contractarian **changes**: Hobbes an irreversible transfer for security, Locke a revocable trust with a right of resistance, Rousseau sovereignty relocated in the general will | Three biographies in sequence, or "all of them said men left the state of nature" |
| Handling Plato and Aristotle | Aristotle wins **comparatively** — he rejects excessive unity and keeps household, property and plurality — while both remain perfectionist and Aristotle retains deep exclusions | Calling Aristotle a liberal individualist, or Plato a totalitarian in the modern sense |
| Using India | One dated constitutional item, classified accurately, with the caution that Fundamental Duties are non-justiciable and that legal status settles no philosophical question | Treating Article 51A as proof that duties are morally prior, or claiming Indian law "recognises civil disobedience" |
| Closing | A **graded verdict** that names the criterion, ranks, and concedes what the losing side secures | "Thus rights and duties are both important for society." |

### HOW TO READ THIS SESSION

1. **Every session opens with a picture.** Read the diagram or table before the prose; the visual carries the argument's shape, and the prose only names its parts.
2. **Read the Basic block straight through.** It is deliberately answer-complete: after Session 20 you can write any of the nine owned previous-year parts without opening the Advanced block.
3. **Do not import the Advanced block into a core answer.** It sits at the end, clearly fenced, and exists for one added debate or one sharper objection in a 15- or 20-marker.
4. **Treat the answer-grabbing line and the paragraph demonstration as drills.** Copy the demonstration's four-move shape — claim → named evidence → analysis → qualification — and refill it in your own words until the shape is automatic.
5. **The consolidated register notes come last on purpose.** They are compressed recall, not a second reading of the teaching.

### ORIGIN AND CHRONOLOGY — THE MINIMUM NEEDED TO AVOID ANACHRONISM

This clause has no single datable origin, because it is a relation rather than a doctrine. What it does have is a **sequence of positions**, and several standing traps in the clause are simply anachronisms or swapped attributions. ⚠️ The rail below fixes the order of the positions and the five provenance dates the paper can punish; it is not a history of institutions.

*Caption: the order of the positions, not a history of states. Five provenance dates and four attribution traps live on this rail.*

⚠️ **The five errors this rail prevents.** (i) **Bentham is not an enemy of rights as such**: he rejects *natural* rights and demands that a right be cashed out in law, which is precisely why he is the sharpest foil to Locke rather than a nihilist about entitlement. (ii) **Thoreau is not Gandhi's blueprint**: Thoreau's 1849 model is individual withdrawal of personal support, closer to conscientious refusal than to an organised public campaign, whereas the 1930 Salt Satyagraha is civil disobedience in its full form. (iii) **Ambedkar's objection is not anti-democratic**: on 25 November 1949 he argued that where constitutional methods of redress are available, unconstitutional methods are unjustified, and he called them the "grammar of anarchy" — an argument about the *availability* of constitutional channels, not a defence of quietism. (iv) ❌ **Never date Dworkin's *Taking Rights Seriously* to any year other than 1977, and never date Article 51A to any amendment other than the Constitution (Forty-second Amendment) Act, 1976.** These are the two most commonly mis-cited provenances in this clause; if unsure in the hall, name the work or the Article without a year. (v) **Hegel and Green must not be flattened into one "idealism"**: Hegel's claim is about rational ethical institutions, not about the sanctity of every incumbent government, and Green's state is a means to the common good that removes hindrances rather than an end that absorbs persons.

### EXACT OWNERSHIP BOUNDARY AND INDISPENSABLE BRIDGES

The printed owner is a dispute about **the justified relation between the person and public power**: what she may claim, what she owes, and why power must answer. Doctrines from neighbouring clauses enter only where an owned previous-year part cannot be answered without them.

| Concept | What this owner must teach | Canonical boundary |
|---|---|---|
| **Sovereignty** | Authority and legitimacy only as they bear on the moral duty to obey or resist | Austin, Bodin, Laski, Kautilya, the command theory and legal finality belong to **Sovereignty** |
| **Anarchism and Marxism** | Both only as *relation-models* — the anarchist denial of a general right to rule, and the Marxist unmasking of formally equal rights | Anarchism, Marxism and socialism as full ideologies belong to **Political Ideologies** |
| **Democracy and constitutional checks** | Constitutional-democratic accountability, minority protection and the protection of dissent | Monarchy, theocracy and democracy as *forms* belong to **Forms of Government** |
| **Liberty, equality and justice** | Negative and positive freedom, the harm principle and the common good only as prerequisites of the relation | The full equality–justice–liberty theories belong to **Social and Political Ideals** |
| **Multiculturalism and gender** | The embedded self, the minority-and-dissent safeguard, and the public/private boundary | Recognition, group rights and gendered private power belong to their later owners |
| **Punishment** | Sanction only as one accountability and obedience mechanism | The theories and applications of punishment belong to **Crime and Punishment** |
| **Jurisprudence** | Dworkin's principles, hard cases and rights as trumps, because they convert rights into constraints on the state | The Austin → Kelsen → Hart sequence is named and routed to **Sovereignty**, never re-expounded here |
| **Indian constitutional law** | Articles 19(1)(a) and 19(1)(b) with their reasonable restrictions, and Article 51A as inserted in 1976 | Article-by-article doctrine and case law belong to **Polity** |

#### The three directions of one relation, and what each direction owes the other two

| Direction | What it asserts | What it needs from the others | Controlling warning |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Rights — the individual claims** | Certain interests, choices and forms of agency must be respected by public power | Duties somewhere in the background, or the claim is socially empty; accountability, or the claim has no remedy | A right is not a wish; it is a *justified* claim, and the justification must be named |
| **Duties — the individual owes** | Coexistence requires obligations of respect, restraint, support and lawful conduct | Rights, or duty becomes servility; accountability, or duty becomes a device for evading answerable power | Duty-talk is legitimate only when it also binds the powerful |
| **Accountability — power answers** | Coercive authority must explain, be judged and be corrected | Rights to define the standard; duties to sustain the institutions that judge | Accountability is neither only elections nor only punishment |

### SESSION 1 — THE PRINTED CLAUSE, THE THREE TERMS AND THE RELATION THAT MUST BE JUSTIFIED

#### The picture first

```text
        WHY THIS CLAUSE EXISTS AT ALL
        =============================

   A CLUB                  A CHURCH                 THE STATE
   you may join            you may leave            you are BORN into it
   you may leave           it may expel you         it CLAIMS the right to
   it fines you only       its sanctions are        COERCE you, jail you,
   if you agreed           spiritual or social      tax you and conscript you
        |                        |                          |
        +------------+-----------+--------------------------+
                     v
        THE STATE IS THE ONE ASSOCIATION THAT CLAIMS TO BIND
        PEOPLE WHO NEVER AGREED TO BE BOUND
                     |
                     v
   THAT CLAIM MUST BE JUSTIFIED -- AND THE JUSTIFICATION HAS THREE PARTS
   +----------------------+----------------------+----------------------+
   | WHAT MAY I CLAIM     | WHAT DO I OWE        | WHY MUST POWER       |
   | AGAINST IT?          | THROUGH IT?          | ANSWER FOR ITSELF?   |
   |     RIGHTS           |     DUTIES           |   ACCOUNTABILITY     |
   +----------------------+----------------------+----------------------+
                     |
                     v
   THE THREE ARE NOT THREE TOPICS -- THEY ARE ONE RELATION, READ THREE WAYS
        rights WITHOUT duties ......... claims with nobody bound = empty
        duties WITHOUT rights ......... obligation with no protection =
                                        subordination
        power WITHOUT accountability .. authority with no answer =
                                        domination
                     |
                     v
   THE FIVE QUESTIONS THE PAPER ACTUALLY SETS ON THIS CLAUSE
     1. WHAT grounds a right?           -> nature / law / morality /
                                           interest / will / dignity
     2. ARE rights and duties LINKED?   -> 2025: "no NECESSARY connection"
     3. WHICH comes FIRST?              -> 2023: duty and accountability
                                           over rights?
     4. WHOM does the state ANSWER to?  -> 2019: do rights make CITIZENS
                                           accountable to the STATE?
     5. WHAT PICTURE of the state?      -> 2024: statism against
                                           individualism
                     |

   *WHY OBEY AT ALL?* -- political obligation (Session 20). If no ground
   binds me, the whole clause collapses into prudence and fear.
```

*Caption: the state is the only association you neither join nor may simply leave, and that single fact is what forces the three printed terms into existence.*

#### In plain words

Every other association in your life rests on something you did. You joined the club, you signed the employment contract, you stayed in the congregation. The state is different. You were born inside it, its laws applied to you before you could read them, and if you break them it will not merely expel you — it will fine you, imprison you, or take your property. No other body claims that.

Once you see that, the syllabus item stops looking like three unrelated words. If an institution claims the right to coerce me, three questions follow immediately and in order. What is it *not* allowed to do to me — that is **rights**. What does it legitimately require *of* me — that is **duties**. And when it does something, who makes it explain itself — that is **accountability**. Three printed terms, one relation, read in three directions.

And notice the trap the paper repeatedly sets. Each of the three is unstable alone. A right that binds nobody is a wish. A duty owed by someone who may claim nothing back is servitude. A power that need answer to no one is domination however efficiently it governs. The strongest single move available on this clause is to refuse to treat any one term in isolation.

#### The doctrine, exactly

✅ **The core statement.** This theme asks how the state can be justified **to** the individual, what the individual may claim **against** the state, what she owes **through** social life, and why public power must remain **answerable**.

✅ **The three printed terms defined.** A **right** is a justified claim, liberty, power or immunity recognised within a moral or legal order. A **duty** is an obligation to do, avoid, respect or support something owed to others, institutions, community or oneself. **Accountability** means that those who exercise public power must explain, justify and bear consequences for their decisions.

✅ **The two poles the relation runs between.** The **individual** is the bearer of agency, dignity, interests and moral status. The **state** is organised public authority claiming binding power. The whole dispute in this clause is whether the person is prior to the state or formed through its institutions.

⚠️ **The working thesis.** Rights protect individuality; duties sustain social order; accountability legitimises coercive power. Any one of the three without the other two becomes distorted: rights without limits may become licence, duties without rights may become subordination, and state power without accountability degenerates into domination. ✅ This single sentence can open or close almost any answer in the clause.

⚠️ **What the clause presupposes.** That coercion needs a justification at all. A theory that treats obedience as natural, divinely commanded or simply given never reaches the three printed questions, because it never admits that the state has a case to answer.

⚠️ **Where the prior question sits.** "Rights, duties and accountability" presupposes an answer to the question *why should anyone obey the state at all?* That is **political obligation**, developed in Session 20. It is not an optional appendix: without it, the duty to obey law is either fear of the sanction or private agreement with the law's content, and in neither case does the law itself add anything to my reasons.

#### Named evidence you can cite

- **Locke** ✅ — rights to life, liberty and property are held in the state of nature; government is a **trust** for their protection and may be forfeited.
- **Bentham** ✅ — natural rights are "nonsense upon stilts"; a right exists where an enforceable legal order supplies a claimant, a duty-bearer and a remedy.
- **Gandhi** ✅ — rights become secure when persons discipline themselves through service, self-restraint and truthfulness; duty is the moral source of right.
- **Article 51A, Constitution of India** ✅ — Fundamental Duties, inserted by the Constitution (Forty-second Amendment) Act, **1976**, and **non-justiciable**.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING LINE — WRITE OR ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> The state is the one association a person neither joins nor may simply leave, and that is precisely why its power has to be justified in three directions at once: as a limit it may not cross (rights), as a claim it may legitimately make (duties), and as an answer it must always be able to give (accountability).

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

**justified claim** · **organised public authority** · **coercion needs justification** · **three directions of one relation** · **licence, subordination, domination** · **political obligation as the prior question** · **answerability, enforceability, responsiveness**

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — claim → named evidence → analysis → qualification

> **Claim.** The three printed terms of this clause are not three subjects but one relation read in three directions, because the state is the only association that claims to bind persons who never agreed to be bound. **Named evidence.** Locke registers the first direction by treating government as a trust that may be forfeited when it violates the rights it was created to protect; the Indian constitutional text registers the second by listing Fundamental Duties in Article 51A, inserted in 1976 and deliberately left non-justiciable; and the whole apparatus of legal and political answerability registers the third. **Analysis.** Each direction is unstable alone: a right nobody is bound to respect is a wish, a duty owed by a person who may claim nothing in return is servitude, and an efficient power that need explain itself to no one is domination rather than authority. **Qualification.** The relation is therefore reciprocal rather than one-directional, though reciprocity does not make the three symmetrical — rights retain political priority because they protect persons against abuse, while duties retain a distinct ethical claim on motivation, and the criterion for ranking them must be named before any ranking is asserted.

#### Objection → reply → residual limit

- **Objection ✅.** The clause is really a civics topic. Rights, duties and accountability are institutional arrangements described by constitutional law, not philosophical problems.
- **Reply ⚠️.** The institutional description presupposes exactly what is disputed: *what justifies* a claim as a right rather than a preference, *what grounds* an obligation as moral rather than merely enforced, and *why* an answer is owed at all. Constitutional law can tell you what a legal order does; it cannot tell you whether the order is legitimate, and legal permission is not moral justification.
- **Residual limit ❓.** The reply does not settle how far philosophy may use legal material at all. A candidate who cites Article 51A is citing an institutional fact, and there is a genuine dispute about whether such facts carry any normative weight. State the caution rather than pretending it has been resolved.

#### Examiner traps on this session

1. Opening with a definition of the **state** or with "man is a social animal" and never returning to the relation. The bearer is not the problem; the justification of its coercion is.
2. Treating rights, duties and accountability as three separate essays stapled together. The mark-bearing move on almost every stem is showing that each collapses without the other two.
3. Answering the general question "are rights important?" instead of the directional question the stem sets — claim against, obligation through, or answerability of.

#### Mini recap

One relation, three directions. Rights are what may be claimed against power, duties what is owed through social life, accountability why power must answer. Each is unstable alone; political obligation is the prior question; and coercion, not sociability, is what makes the whole clause necessary.

---

### SESSION 2 — TWO RIVAL PICTURES: MECHANISTIC AGAINST ORGANIC, AND THE SIX RELATION-MODELS

#### The picture first

```text
   THE PICTURE YOU CHOOSE DECIDES EVERY VERDICT YOU WILL LATER GIVE
   ================================================================

   MECHANISTIC / ARTIFICIAL                 ORGANIC / ETHICAL
   ------------------------                 -----------------
   persons are LOGICALLY PRIOR              the political WHOLE is prior to
   the state is an INSTRUMENT               the fully developed citizen
   made for security, rights,               persons become fully human
   or cooperation                           WITHIN a natural or ethical whole
        |                                        |
   ORIGIN: artificial agreement,            ORIGIN: natural growth,
   convention, instrument                   historical development,
        |                                   ethical life
   FREEDOM = a protected sphere                  |
   AGAINST interference                     FREEDOM = participation in
        |                                   rational institutions and the
   RIGHTS: strong rights AGAINST            common good
   the state are intelligible                    |
        |                                   RIGHTS: mature WITHIN an
   NAMES: Hobbes, Locke,                    ethical / legal order
   contractarian liberalism                      |
   (with SHARPLY different                  NAMES: Aristotle (natural polis),
   contracts)                               Hegel (ethical state), Green
        |                                        |

   hollow FORMAL freedom                    silencing of conscience

   +--------------------------------------------------------------------+
   |  CONTROL: these are RIVAL LOGICAL METAPHORS about EXPLANATORY       |
   |  PRIORITY -- not literal biology and not literal engineering.       |
   |  Mechanistic = the state is a MEANS to prior individual ends.       |
   |  Organic = the whole is PARTLY CONSTITUTIVE of the person's good.   |
   +--------------------------------------------------------------------+

   THE SIX RELATION-MODELS ON ONE GRID
   MODEL          IMAGE OF THE RELATION        FREEDOM IS...     MAIN DANGER
   -------------  ---------------------------  ----------------  --------------
   MECHANISTIC    instrument created by prior  a protected       atomism;
                  persons                      sphere            hollow freedom
   ORGANIC        whole partly constitutes     participation in  statism;
                  the person's good            rational life     silenced
                                                                 conscience
   WELFARE /      instrumental, but must       effective         paternalism;
   POSITIVE       remove social hindrances     CAPACITY, not     bureaucratic
   LIBERAL        to agency                    only absence of   dependence
                                               restraint
   MARXIST        class state universalises    emancipation      under-protected
                  a PARTIAL interest;          from material     civil liberty in
                  expected to disappear        domination        transition
   ANARCHIST      coercive authority has NO    autonomous        coordination,
                  demonstrated general         voluntary         adjudication and
                  right to rule                cooperation       security deficits

                  EMBEDDED; politics may       autonomy within   suppression of
                  sustain shared goods         relationships     internal minorities
                                    |
                                    v
   THE QUALIFIED SYNTHESIS THAT WINS MARKS
   Modern constitutional democracy is MECHANISTIC in insisting that
   institutions serve persons, and ORGANIC in recognising that agency,
   education and citizenship are SOCIALLY FORMED. Neither metaphor is a
   complete theory -- and saying so is an argument, not a compromise.
```

*Caption: six pictures of one relation. Every later verdict in this clause — on rights, on duty-priority, on statism, on obedience — is already implied by the picture you pick here.*

#### In plain words

Ask two people what the state *is*, and you will get two incompatible pictures. The first says: people came first, they had purposes of their own, and they built the state as a tool to protect those purposes. On that picture the state is like a machine — assembled, serviceable, replaceable — and freedom means a space the machine may not enter.

The second says: nobody becomes a full human being alone. Language, education, law, family and civic life are what turn a biological individual into a person capable of choosing anything at all. On that picture the political community is not a tool added to finished persons; it is part of what makes them persons. Freedom then means participating well in institutions that are rational, not merely being left alone.

Neither picture is stupid, and the exam does not want you to pick one and shout. It wants you to see that four further positions grow out of the tension between them — the welfare liberal who keeps the instrument but insists it must remove obstacles, the Marxist who says the instrument secretly serves a class, the anarchist who says the instrument has never proved its right to coerce, and the communitarian who says the atomised person of the first picture never existed.

#### The doctrine, exactly

✅ **The mechanistic or artificial view.** Individuals are logically prior; the state is an instrument created for security, rights or cooperation. Its origin is artificial agreement, convention or instrumentality; freedom is a protected sphere against interference; and strong rights **against** state power are intelligible. Paradigm names are Hobbes and Locke and contractarian liberalism — ⚠️ with sharply different contracts, so the shared label must never be allowed to blur them.

✅ **The organic or ethical view.** The political whole is logically prior to the fully developed citizen. Its origin is natural growth, historical development or ethical life; the state's role is to constitute conditions of the good life and moral freedom; and rights mature **within** an ethical or legal order rather than existing as pre-social atoms. Paradigm names are Aristotle as natural-polis theorist and Hegel as ethical-state theorist.

✅ **The control that prevents the standard error.** The distinction concerns **logical structure, not literal biology or engineering**. Mechanistic theory treats the state as a means to prior individual ends; organic theory treats the political whole as partly constitutive of the person's good. ❌ Do not write that organic theorists believed the state is literally an animal.

✅ **The four further relation-models.** The **welfare or positive liberal** state remains instrumental but must remove social hindrances to agency, so freedom becomes effective capacity as well as non-interference. The **Marxist** model treats the class state as presenting a partial interest as universal, with emancipation from material domination as the goal. The **anarchist** model denies that coercive authority has a demonstrated general right to rule and prefers autonomous voluntary cooperation. The **communitarian** model holds that the self is socially embedded and that political life may sustain shared goods, so autonomy is situated within relationships and practices.

⚠️ **The risks, stated symmetrically.** Mechanistic thinking risks atomism, unchecked private power and hollow formal freedom. Organic thinking risks statism, fixed hierarchy and the silencing of conscience. Welfare provision risks paternalism and dependence. Marxist transition risks under-protecting civil liberty. Anarchism risks deficits in coordination, adjudication and external security. Communitarianism risks conformity and the suppression of internal minorities.

⚠️ **The qualified synthesis.** Modern constitutional democracy is mechanistic in insisting that institutions serve persons, but organic in recognising that agency, education and citizenship are socially formed. Neither metaphor is a complete theory. ✅ This is the safest closing position for any stem that asks about statism, individualism or the "nature" of the state.

#### Named evidence you can cite

- **Gauba, *An Introduction to Political Theory*** ✅ — the searchable local text supplies the organic, liberal-individualist, welfare, class, communitarian, Gandhian and pluralist taxonomy at pp. 220–279.
- **Aristotle** ✅ — the polis is natural and exists for the good life; the organic pole in its classical form.
- **Hegel** ✅ — freedom becomes concrete through recognised rights and participation in rational institutions differentiated into family, civil society and state.
- **T. H. Green** ✅ — the state is a means to the common good whose proper work is to remove remediable hindrances to self-realisation.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING LINE — WRITE OR ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> The dispute between the mechanistic and the organic picture is not about whether the state is useful but about **explanatory priority** — whether persons with settled ends build institutions, or institutions partly constitute the persons who then have ends at all; and modern constitutionalism is unstable precisely because it needs both halves of that answer.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

**explanatory priority** · **artificial agreement against natural growth** · **instrument against constitutive whole** · **rights against the state / rights within an order** · **atomism against statism** · **six relation-models** · **rival logical metaphors, not biology**

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — claim → named evidence → analysis → qualification

> **Claim.** The individual–state relation is contested at the level of explanatory priority, and the mechanistic and organic pictures name the two poles between which every later position in this clause is located. **Named evidence.** The mechanistic pole runs through Hobbes and Locke and the contractarian tradition, in which the state is an instrument created by prior persons for security or the protection of rights; the organic pole runs through Aristotle's natural polis and Hegel's ethical state, in which the whole is partly constitutive of the citizen's good, with Green supplying its most defensible form as the removal of hindrances to self-realisation. **Analysis.** Because the two pictures answer a question about logical structure rather than about biology, they generate opposite risks rather than opposite facts: the instrumentalist under-describes how agency is socially produced and so tolerates private domination, while the organicist over-describes the state's constitutive role and so licenses coercion in the name of a higher self. **Qualification.** A constitutional democracy is therefore best read as mechanistic in its insistence that institutions serve persons and organic in its recognition that citizenship is formed rather than found — which is a substantive position, not a refusal to choose, because it fixes the direction of justification while conceding the social formation of the chooser.

#### Objection → reply → residual limit

- **Objection ✅.** The organic view is simply authoritarianism with better vocabulary. Once the state is said to constitute the person's good, any coercion can be redescribed as liberation.
- **Reply ⚠️.** That is a fair objection to the crude version and is exactly why Hegel and Green must not be flattened. The defensible idealist reconstruction makes enabling action rights-bound, decentralised where possible and open to public challenge, and Green explicitly leaves moral agency with citizens rather than transferring it to officials.
- **Residual limit ❓.** The reply does not fully dissolve the danger. Once anyone is authorised to specify the "rational" or "higher" self, the criterion for distinguishing enabling from imposition remains contested, and the dispute survives the reply.

#### Examiner traps on this session

1. Writing the organic theory as a literal biological claim. It is a claim about logical priority; the biology is a metaphor and treating it as doctrine invites easy refutation.
2. Merging Hobbes and Locke because both are "contractarians". Hobbes's transfer is irreversible and security-driven; Locke's is a revocable trust with a right of resistance. The 2024 Q1(b) part is failed by exactly this merger.
3. Presenting the synthesis as a refusal to decide. State the direction of justification — institutions serve persons — and then concede the social formation of agency; that is a position, not a fence-sit.

#### Mini recap

Two pictures, six models, one axis: explanatory priority. Mechanistic risks atomism, organic risks statism, welfare risks paternalism, Marxism risks liberty in transition, anarchism risks coordination, communitarianism risks conformity. Constitutional democracy needs both poles and can defend needing both.

### SESSION 3 — RIGHTS I: WHAT A RIGHT IS, AND THE GROUNDS THAT JUSTIFY IT

#### The picture first

```text
   FROM WANT TO RIGHT -- THE LADDER THAT MUST BE CLIMBED
   =====================================================

   DESIRE / PREFERENCE          "I want it."
        |                        no normative standing whatever
        v
   NEED                         "I cannot flourish without it."
        |                        urgent -- but urgency alone is not
        |                        entitlement
        v
   INTEREST WORTH PROTECTING    "This matters enough that others'
        |                        conduct should be constrained."
        v
   JUSTIFIED CLAIM = A RIGHT    "I am OWED this, and someone is
                                 BOUND accordingly."
                     |
                     v
   WHAT A RIGHT IS, EXACTLY
   a JUSTIFIED claim, liberty, power or immunity recognised within a
   MORAL or LEGAL order -- a PROTECTED NORMATIVE POSITION
                     |
   +-----------------+------------------------------------------+
   |  THE FOUR THINGS A RIGHT-CLAIM MUST SUPPLY                  |
   |  1. a HOLDER      -- whose right is it?                     |
   |  2. a CONTENT     -- to do / to receive / to be free from   |
   |  3. a BEARER      -- who is constrained, and how?           |
   |  4. a GROUND      -- WHY is this claim justified?           |
   +-------------------------------------------------------------+
                     |
                     v
   THE COMPETING GROUNDS -- THIS IS THE WHOLE OF SESSIONS 4 TO 6
   GROUND        THE CLAIM                       SESSION
   -----------   ------------------------------  -------
   NATURE        rights precede the state and     S4  (Locke)
                 constrain it
   LAW           rights exist where an enforce-   S4  (Bentham)
                 able order names claimant,
                 duty-bearer and remedy
   MORALITY      rights exist wherever ethical    S4  (moral rights)
                 reasoning justifies them, even
                 before law
   WILL          rights protect CHOICE and        S6
                 control over another's duty
   INTEREST      rights protect IMPORTANT         S6
                 INTERESTS necessary to
                 well-being
   DIGNITY       rights belong to all human       S7  (human rights)
                 beings simply as human
                     |
                     v
   THREE DISTINCTIONS TO WRITE IN THE FIRST PARAGRAPH
   NEED vs RIGHT ....... a need may be urgent and still not be a right
   LIBERTY vs CLAIM .... liberty = non-interference; claim-right =
                         REQUIRES correlative conduct from others
   MORAL vs LEGAL ...... a moral right may exist WITHOUT enforcement;
                         a legal right exists WITHIN an institutional order
```

*Caption: rights-talk becomes precise the moment you demand four things of every claim — holder, content, bearer and ground — and the ground is what all the theories in Sessions 4 to 7 are fighting about.*

#### In plain words

The word "right" is the most inflated word in political argument. People say they have a right to a job, a right to be respected, a right to a good mood on Monday. If everything wanted is a right, then nothing claimed as a right carries weight, and the concept dies of overuse.

Philosophy repairs this not by shouting "that is not a right" but by asking four questions of any claim. Whose right is it? What exactly is its content? Who is bound, and to do what? And on what ground is the claim justified rather than merely felt? A claim that can answer all four is a right. A claim that cannot answer the fourth is a desire wearing a borrowed suit.

Notice that the fourth question is where the whole clause lives. Everyone can agree that a right is a justified claim; the fight is entirely over what *counts* as a justification. Nature says the ground is prior moral status. Law says the ground is enactment. Morality says the ground is ethical reasoning independent of enactment. Will theory says the ground is control. Interest theory says the ground is important interests. Human-rights theory says the ground is humanity itself. Sessions 4 to 7 are those six answers, in order.

#### The doctrine, exactly

✅ **The core statement.** A right is not merely a desire or preference; it is a **justified claim, liberty, power or immunity recognised within a moral or legal order**. It marks a **protected normative position**: some interests, choices or forms of agency deserve respect.

✅ **The question a theory of rights answers.** *What is owed to a person because she is a person, citizen, or member of a community?*

✅ **The presupposition.** Rights presuppose some standard of justification — nature, law, morality, utility, dignity, interest, autonomy, or social recognition. ⚠️ Naming which standard the answer is using is the single cheapest way to look disciplined in the first paragraph.

✅ **The three distinctions that discipline the term.** (i) A **need** may be urgent, but not every need is yet a right. (ii) A **liberty** means non-interference; a **claim-right** requires correlative conduct from others. (iii) A **moral right** may exist even without legal enforcement, while a **legal right** exists within an institutional order.

⚠️ **Why the concept resists inflation on its own.** Because a right is *justified*, it comes with a burden of argument. The claim "I have a right to X" always invites the counter-question "on what ground, and who is thereby bound?" — and most inflated claims collapse at the second half of that question, since nobody can say who is bound or to do what.

#### Named evidence you can cite

- **Hohfeld** ✅ — the analytic scheme that separates claim, liberty, power and immunity, and so supplies the conceptual discipline the inflation objection demands (Session 5).
- **Locke** ✅ — the natural-rights ground: rights belong to persons prior to and independent of the state.
- **Bentham** ✅ — the legal ground: a right exists where an enforceable legal system identifies claimant, duty-bearer and remedy.
- **The interest and will theories** ✅ — the two modern grounds, tested against the hard cases of children and the severely disabled (Session 6).

#### ANSWER-GRABBING LINE — WRITE OR ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> A right is not a want with better manners: it is a **justified** claim, and the entire dispute in this clause is about what counts as the justification — nature, enactment, moral reasoning, autonomous choice, protected interest, or humanity as such.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

**justified claim** · **protected normative position** · **holder, content, bearer, ground** · **need is not yet a right** · **liberty against claim-right** · **moral right against legal right** · **standard of justification must be named**

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — claim → named evidence → analysis → qualification

> **Claim.** A right is a justified claim, liberty, power or immunity recognised within a moral or legal order, and the word does real work only when the justification is named. **Named evidence.** Locke grounds rights in a moral status prior to political authority; Bentham insists that a right exists only where an enforceable order supplies a claimant, a duty-bearer and a remedy; and Hohfeld supplies the analytic apparatus that separates a claim-right, which is strictly correlative to a duty, from a liberty, which is not. **Analysis.** These are not three descriptions of one thing but three different answers to the question of ground, and the practical test that distinguishes a right from an inflated preference is whether the claimant can say who is bound and to do what — a test most inflated claims fail on their second half. **Qualification.** The objection that rights-talk has become vague is therefore well-taken as a criticism of usage rather than of the concept: conceptual discipline is available, but it has to be applied, and a candidate who asserts a right without naming its ground has conceded the objection.

#### Objection → reply → residual limit

- **Objection ❓.** Rights-talk is vague and inflated; modern discourse calls every preference a right, so the concept has lost all discriminating power.
- **Reply ✅.** Conceptual discipline is supplied by theories such as Hohfeld's analysis, which separates claim, liberty, power and immunity and specifies the correlative of each. Inflation is a failure of users, not of the concept.
- **Residual limit ❓.** The reply is analytic, and analysis does not by itself tell you which interests are important enough to ground a claim. Hohfeld clarifies the *form* of a right without settling its *content*, so the inflation problem returns at the level of justification and must be met there.

#### Examiner traps on this session

1. Defining a right as "what every human deserves by birth". That is one contested answer — the natural-rights ground — smuggled in as if it were the definition.
2. Forgetting that a liberty is a right. The word covers four different normative positions, and only the claim-right is strictly correlative to a duty; the 2025 part turns on precisely this.
3. Treating the inflation objection as fatal. It is answerable by analysis, but the answer is only analytic — say so, and the paragraph reads as philosophy rather than as slogan.

#### Mini recap

Four demands of every claim: holder, content, bearer, ground. Six competing grounds: nature, law, morality, will, interest, dignity. Three distinctions to open with: need against right, liberty against claim, moral against legal.

---

### SESSION 4 — RIGHTS II: NATURAL RIGHTS (LOCKE), LEGAL RIGHTS (BENTHAM), AND MORAL RIGHTS

#### The picture first

```text
   THREE ANSWERS TO "WHAT MAKES A CLAIM A RIGHT?"
   ==============================================

   NATURAL RIGHTS                 LEGAL / POSITIVIST          MORAL RIGHTS
   (LOCKE)                        (BENTHAM)                   (the residue)
   ---------------                ------------------          -------------
   SOURCE                         SOURCE                      SOURCE
   rights belong to persons       rights exist where LAW      ethical reasoning
   IN THE STATE OF NATURE,        recognises them             independent of
   before any sovereign                 |                     enactment
        |                         REQUIRES                         |
   CONTENT                        a CLAIMANT + a DUTY-        FUNCTION
   life, liberty, property        BEARER + a REMEDY           lets us say the law
        |                              |                      OUGHT to recognise
   FUNCTION OF THE STATE          FAMOUS LINE                 a right it does not
   FIDUCIARY protection --        natural rights are               |
   government is a TRUST          "NONSENSE UPON STILTS"      EXAMPLES OF ITS WORK
        |                              |                      anti-slavery,
   IF THE TRUST IS VIOLATED       WHAT HE IS *NOT* SAYING     anti-colonial and
   RESISTANCE is justified        he does NOT reject ALL      anti-discrimination
        |                         rights -- he rejects        claims are intelligible
   UPSHOT                         rights WITHOUT LAW          BEFORE legal recognition
   government is MORALLY               |
   LIMITED and cannot define      UPSHOT
   justice by command             clear institutions beat
                                  metaphysical assertion

   +--------------------------------------------------------------------+
   |  THE TWO OBJECTIONS, FACING EACH OTHER                              |
   |  AGAINST LOCKE: natural rights cannot be empirically shown, and     |
   |    often encode historically specific PROPERTY interests.           |
   |  AGAINST BENTHAM: if LAW ALONE creates rights, an oppressive legal  |
   |    system could DEFINE INJUSTICE AS JUSTICE.                        |
   |  THE MORAL-RIGHTS ANSWER: legal VALIDITY and moral LEGITIMACY are   |
   |    not identical -- which is exactly the space moral rights occupy. |
   +--------------------------------------------------------------------+

   LOCKE AGAINST HOBBES -- DO NOT MERGE THEM
   HOBBES                                LOCKE
   begins with SELF-PRESERVATION and     gives the state of nature a more
   extensive natural liberty UNDER FEAR  NORM-GOVERNED structure
   contract -> irreversible transfer     contract -> a TRUST, revocable
   sovereign command secures PEACE       limited government secures RIGHTS
   no right of resistance in the         RESISTANCE justified when the
   ordinary case                         trust is violated
```

*Caption: three grounds, two objections that mirror each other, and the Locke–Hobbes separation that the 2024 origin-and-development part is failed by ignoring.*

#### In plain words

Locke's move is simple and enormous. He says you had rights before there was a government, which means the government did not give them to you and therefore cannot simply take them away. Political society exists to *secure* rights, not to create them out of nothing. And if the government breaks that trust, the trust is broken — resistance becomes justified, because the whole point of the arrangement has failed.

Bentham's reply is equally simple and equally enormous. Show me, he says, the right. Where is it written, who is bound by it, and what happens if they ignore it? A right you cannot cash out in an enforceable legal relation is not a modest right; it is a rhetorical flourish, and a dangerous one, because everyone can assert one and nobody can adjudicate between them. Natural rights, he says, are "nonsense upon stilts".

The obvious question then arrives on its own: if only law creates rights, what do we say about a legal system that authorises slavery? Bentham has an answer as a reformer — change the law — but the *language* in which we say the law ought to change is not itself legal. That language is moral rights, and it is why the third column exists.

#### The doctrine, exactly

✅ **Natural rights — the statement.** Certain rights belong to persons **prior to, and independent of, the state**. In Locke, individuals are by nature free and equal, and possess rights not because a sovereign grants them but because their moral standing precedes political authority. Political society is created to secure — not create ex nihilo — those rights.

| Element | Locke's position | UPSC note |
|---|---|---|
| Source | ✅ rights belong to persons in the state of nature | Pre-political grounding of rights |
| Content | ✅ life, liberty, property | The standard triad for an introduction |
| Function of state | ✅ fiduciary protection of rights | Limited government |
| Right to resistance | ✅ if government violates the trust, resistance is justified | Rights as a check on sovereignty |

✅ **The presupposition.** Human beings possess an intrinsic moral status not reducible to convention or positive law.

✅ **Locke against Hobbes.** Hobbes begins with self-preservation and extensive natural liberty under fear; Locke gives the state of nature a more norm-governed structure and treats rights as morally prior constraints on government. ❌ Merging them destroys the 2024 Q1(b) answer.

✅ **Legal or positivist theory — the statement.** Rights are identified with **recognition by law and institutions**. Bentham dismisses natural rights as "nonsense upon stilts". ⚠️ His point is not that all rights are absurd but that rights without law are rhetorically inflated and politically dangerous; on this view a right exists where an enforceable legal system identifies a **claimant, a duty-bearer and a remedy**.

✅ **The positivist presupposition and distinction.** Clear institutions matter more than metaphysical assertion; rights require codification, specification and enforcement. Bentham rejects **natural** rights, not **all** rights. Positivism asks *what rights exist in law?*; moral-rights theories ask *what rights ought to exist even before law recognises them?*

✅ **Moral rights — the statement.** Moral rights are justified claims grounded in **ethical reasoning rather than merely in legal enactment**. This discourse allows critique of unjust law and explains how anti-slavery, anti-colonial or anti-discrimination claims are intelligible before legal recognition. Moral rights may later become legal rights, but they are not reducible to them.

⚠️ **The controlling relation between the three.** Legal validity and moral legitimacy are **not identical**. That single sentence is what allows Locke's objection to Bentham and Bentham's objection to Locke to be true at the same time: the first shows that law needs an external standard, the second shows that a standard without institutions cannot be enforced.

#### Named evidence you can cite

- **Locke, *Two Treatises of Government*** ✅ — life, liberty and estate held in the state of nature; government as a trust that may be forfeited. ⚠️ Limitation: the theological and property premises are not available to a secular reader without reconstruction.
- **Bentham** ✅ — "nonsense upon stilts"; rights as children of law. ⚠️ Limitation: the position cannot explain the critical use of rights language *against* existing law.
- **Anti-slavery and anti-colonial claims** ✅ — the standard illustration that a right can be argued for before it is enacted.
- **Hobbes, *Leviathan*** ✅ — the contrast case that keeps Locke's contract distinct.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING LINE — WRITE OR ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Bentham did not deny rights; he denied rights **without law** — and the reason his challenge cannot be the last word is that the sentence "this law violates a right" is one every reformer must be able to utter, and no purely legal theory can supply its grammar.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

**pre-political moral standing** · **fiduciary trust** · **forfeiture and resistance** · **nonsense upon stilts** · **claimant, duty-bearer, remedy** · **rights as children of law** · **legal validity is not moral legitimacy** · **Locke is not Hobbes**

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — claim → named evidence → analysis → qualification

> **Claim.** The dispute between natural and legal theories of rights is a dispute about ground, and each position is strongest exactly where the other is weakest. **Named evidence.** Locke holds that persons possess life, liberty and property in the state of nature, so that government is a fiduciary trust which may be forfeited and resisted when it violates the rights it was instituted to secure; Bentham replies that a right exists only where an enforceable order supplies a claimant, a duty-bearer and a remedy, and dismisses natural rights as "nonsense upon stilts". **Analysis.** Locke's account explains why an unjust statute can be *criticised* as a violation rather than merely disliked, which no purely positivist account can do; Bentham's account explains why a right must be specifiable and enforceable, which no purely metaphysical account can do — and the residue between them is precisely the space that moral rights occupy, since anti-slavery and anti-colonial claims were intelligible before any legal order recognised them. **Qualification.** The two therefore need not be ranked but must be kept distinct: legal validity and moral legitimacy are separate questions, and a theory that collapses them either licenses the oppressive legal system Bentham's critics fear or leaves Locke's rights without any institution able to vindicate them.

#### Objection → reply → residual limit

- **Objection ❓.** Natural rights cannot be empirically shown and often hide historically specific property interests.
- **Reply ⚠️.** Even if not empirically observable, natural-rights language expresses the idea that government is **morally limited** and cannot define justice solely by command. The claim is normative, and demanding empirical proof of a normative claim is a category error.
- **Residual limit ❓.** The historical objection survives the reply. Locke's specific content — especially property — carries seventeenth-century premises, and a candidate who deploys Locke should reconstruct the argument's structure rather than importing its content unexamined.

#### Examiner traps on this session

1. Writing that Bentham rejected all rights. ✅ He rejects **natural** rights, not legal rights as such — and the misstatement makes the 2025 correlativity part unanswerable, because his positivism is the strongest reason for thinking rights and duties are institutionally linked.
2. Merging Locke's contract with Hobbes's. The transfer, the limits and the right of resistance all differ, and 2024 Q1(b) rewards exactly that difference.
3. Treating moral rights as a vague third option. They have a precise function: they name the standard by which a valid law can be criticised, which is why legal validity and moral legitimacy must be kept apart.

#### Mini recap

Three grounds: nature, law, morality. Locke gives pre-political standing, fiduciary trust and a right of resistance. Bentham demands claimant, duty-bearer and remedy, and rejects natural rights only. Moral rights hold the space in which an unjust law can still be called unjust.

---

### SESSION 5 — RIGHTS III: HOHFELD'S FOUR INCIDENTS

#### The picture first

```text
   "A RIGHT" IS NOT ONE THING -- IT IS FOUR JURIDICAL RELATIONS
   ============================================================

   INCIDENT       CORRELATIVE   OPPOSITE     WHAT IT ACTUALLY MEANS
   ------------   -----------   ----------   ---------------------------
   CLAIM-RIGHT    DUTY          NO-RIGHT     someone else is BOUND to
                                             act or forbear for me
   LIBERTY /      NO-RIGHT      DUTY         I am FREE to act; nobody is
   PRIVILEGE                                 bound to help me do it
   POWER          LIABILITY     DISABILITY   I can ALTER legal relations
   IMMUNITY       DISABILITY    LIABILITY    others CANNOT alter my legal
                                             position

   READ EACH ROW AS A PAIR -- THIS IS THE WHOLE APPARATUS
   +----------------------------------------------------------------+
   |  IF I HAVE A CLAIM-RIGHT   -> YOU HAVE A DUTY                   |
   |  IF I HAVE A LIBERTY       -> YOU HAVE NO-RIGHT that I desist   |
   |  IF I HAVE A POWER         -> YOU ARE LIABLE to the change      |
   |  IF I HAVE AN IMMUNITY     -> YOU ARE DISABLED from changing it |
   +----------------------------------------------------------------+
                          |
                          v
   ONE ORDINARY "RIGHT" USUALLY BUNDLES SEVERAL INCIDENTS
   FREEDOM OF SPEECH, UNPACKED
     LIBERTY ...... I may speak; nobody is bound to make me speak
     CLAIM-RIGHT .. against censorship in specified contexts
     IMMUNITY ..... against arbitrary alteration of the protection
     (and note) ... NO general claim-right that others PROVIDE me a
                    platform, an audience or a printing press
                          |
                          v
   WHY THIS DECIDES THE 2025 QUESTION
   "Duties are obligation, Rights are entitlement, therefore NO
    NECESSARY connection."
     -> CORRELATIVITY IS STRONGEST for CLAIM-RIGHTS: strictly correlative
     -> CORRELATIVITY IS WEAK / ABSENT for LIBERTIES: a liberty imposes
        NO positive duty to assist
     -> SO the modal word "necessarily" is TRUE for one class and FALSE
        for another -- and THAT split IS the answer
                          |
                          v
   WHAT HOHFELD DOES AND DOES NOT DO
   DOES ..... prevents category mistakes; shows that many disputes are
              verbal because "right" is used equivocally
   DOES NOT.. justify any right; it is an ANALYTIC scheme, not a MORAL
              theory -- so it cannot tell you WHICH interests matter
```

*Caption: four incidents, four correlatives, four opposites. This is the single most reusable analytic instrument in the whole clause, and it decides the modal word in the 2025 part.*

#### In plain words

Hohfeld noticed that lawyers and philosophers were using one word for four different things and then arguing about the resulting confusion. His fix is a table, and the table has genuine power because each row comes in a pair. If I have a claim-right, that is *the same fact* described from your side as your duty. If I merely have a liberty, that is the same fact described from your side as your having no right to stop me — but you are not thereby obliged to help.

Take freedom of speech. It is not one relation, it is a bundle. I have a liberty to speak; you have no right that I keep silent. In specified contexts I have a claim-right against censorship; officials have a duty not to censor. I have an immunity against arbitrary alteration of that protection; ordinary officials are disabled from changing it at will. But I do *not* have a general claim-right that you provide me a platform. Every one of those is a different Hohfeldian position, and running them together is how most bad rights arguments begin.

That is why this session, which looks technical, is the decisive one for the 2025 question. Once you can say "correlativity is strict for claim-rights and absent for liberties", the word *necessarily* in that stem has been analysed instead of merely denied.

#### The doctrine, exactly

✅ **The core statement.** "Right" is **not a single simple thing** but a cluster of juridical relations, and Hohfeld distinguishes four **incidents**, each with its own correlative and its own opposite.

| Hohfeldian incident | Correlative | Opposite | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Claim-right** | duty | no-right | the strongest basis for any rights–duties discussion |
| **Liberty / privilege** | no-right | duty | freedom to act, not always a claim on others |
| **Power** | liability | disability | ability to alter legal relations |
| **Immunity** | disability | liability | protection against alteration by others |

✅ **What the scheme prevents.** It stops confusion in philosophical and exam writing. Saying "I have a right" may name very different normative structures: a **liberty** does not automatically impose a positive duty to help me exercise it, whereas a **claim-right** does.

⚠️ **The bundling point.** A person's right to free speech can simultaneously include a liberty to speak, a claim against censorship in certain contexts, and an immunity from arbitrary alteration by ordinary power. ✅ Correlativity is **strongest in claim-rights but not identical in all usages** of the word "right".

✅ **The presupposition.** Precision in legal-philosophical language avoids category mistakes. Many false debates arise from ambiguous use of a single word.

⚠️ **The limit of the scheme, stated honestly.** Hohfeld supplies an **analytic** apparatus, not a **moral justification**. It tells you the shape a right must have; it cannot tell you which interests are important enough to deserve one. That is why Sessions 6 and 7 are needed after it.

#### Named evidence you can cite

- **Hohfeld, *Fundamental Legal Conceptions*** ✅ — the four incidents with their correlatives and opposites. ⚠️ Limitation: an analytic scheme, not a moral justification.
- **Freedom of speech as a bundle** ⚠️ — liberty, claim-right and immunity in one ordinary "right", with no general claim-right to a platform.
- **The 2025 stem** ✅ — "Duties are of the nature of obligation while Rights are of the nature of entitlement. Therefore there is no necessary connection between the two."

#### ANSWER-GRABBING LINE — WRITE OR ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Hohfeld's contribution is not a refinement but a repair: once "right" is resolved into claim, liberty, power and immunity, the question "are rights and duties necessarily connected?" stops being a slogan-contest and becomes answerable — **strictly, for claim-rights; not at all, for liberties**.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

**four incidents** · **claim-right ↔ duty** · **liberty ↔ no-right** · **power ↔ liability** · **immunity ↔ disability** · **bundling** · **category mistake** · **analytic scheme, not moral justification**

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — claim → named evidence → analysis → qualification

> **Claim.** The word "right" names four distinct juridical positions, and most disputes about rights and duties are verbal until those positions are separated. **Named evidence.** Hohfeld's *Fundamental Legal Conceptions* distinguishes the claim-right, whose correlative is a duty; the liberty or privilege, whose correlative is merely a no-right in others; the power, whose correlative is liability; and the immunity, whose correlative is disability — and an ordinary right such as freedom of speech bundles a liberty, a contextual claim-right and an immunity at once. **Analysis.** The immediate payoff is that correlativity, which is often asserted as a universal law, holds strictly only for claim-rights: a liberty to act imposes on others no positive duty to assist, so the general thesis that every right entails a duty is false as stated and true as restricted. **Qualification.** The scheme nevertheless settles form rather than content — it is analytic, not normative — so it cannot by itself tell us which interests deserve protection, and a complete answer must move on to the will and interest theories that attempt exactly that justification.

#### Objection → reply → residual limit

- **Objection ❓.** Hohfeld is too technical for normative political theory; a jurist's table cannot decide questions about dignity and freedom.
- **Reply ✅.** Technical clarity is philosophically useful precisely because **many false debates arise from ambiguous use of "right"**. The table does not replace normative argument; it prevents normative argument from being wasted on equivocation.
- **Residual limit ❓.** The reply concedes the deeper point. Analysis is preparatory, and the substantive questions — which interests ground claims, and how conflicts between them are settled — remain entirely open after the table has done its work.

#### Examiner traps on this session

1. Reducing Hohfeld to one formula. ✅ He analyses **four** incidents, only one of which is strictly correlated with a duty; reciting only the claim-right half is the standard loss of marks.
2. Reading the correlatives as moral claims. They are relational descriptions: "no-right" is not a criticism of anyone, it simply states that nobody may demand that I desist.
3. Using Hohfeld and then still writing "rights and duties are two sides of the same coin". The table was introduced precisely to replace that sentence with a distinction.

#### Mini recap

Claim-right ↔ duty. Liberty ↔ no-right. Power ↔ liability. Immunity ↔ disability. Ordinary rights bundle several incidents. Correlativity is strict for claim-rights and absent for liberties — and that split is the 2025 answer. The scheme fixes form, not content.

---
### SESSION 6 — RIGHTS IV: WILL THEORY AGAINST INTEREST THEORY

#### The picture first

```text
   WHAT IS A RIGHT *FOR*? -- TWO ANSWERS AND THEIR HARD CASES
   ==========================================================

   WILL THEORY (CHOICE THEORY)          INTEREST THEORY (BENEFIT THEORY)
   ---------------------------          -------------------------------
   A right = NORMATIVE CONTROL          A right = PROTECTION of an
   over another person's DUTY           IMPORTANT INTEREST necessary
        |                               for well-being
   THE RIGHT-HOLDER IS A                     |
   "SMALL SOVEREIGN": she may            THE RIGHT-HOLDER NEED NOT
   WAIVE, ENFORCE or DEMAND              CHOOSE ANYTHING at all
        |                                     |
   FITS: autonomy, consent,              FITS: welfare rights, protective
   contract, liberal emphasis            rights, rights of those who
   on self-determination                 cannot yet exercise choice
        |                                     |
   ASKS: WHO CONTROLS the duty?          ASKS: WHOSE IMPORTANT INTEREST
                                         JUSTIFIES imposing the duty?

   +--------------------------------------------------------------------+
   |  THE HARD CASES -- EACH THEORY BREAKS ON THE OTHER'S STRONG CASE    |
   |                                                                     |
   |  CHILD / SEVERELY DISABLED PERSON / INFANT                          |
   |    WILL THEORY struggles: they cannot waive or enforce, so on a     |
   |    strict reading they hold no rights -- which is intolerable       |
   |    INTEREST THEORY handles easily: their interests plainly ground   |
   |    duties in others                                                 |
   |                                                                     |
   |  THE CONTRACTING ADULT WHO CHOOSES TO WAIVE                         |
   |    WILL THEORY handles easily: waiver IS the exercise of the right  |
   |    INTEREST THEORY struggles: if the right protects an interest,    |
   |    why may the holder discard the protection?                       |
   +--------------------------------------------------------------------+
                          |
                          v
   THE MIXED POSITION MOST MODERN SYSTEMS ACTUALLY OCCUPY
   rights BOTH secure agency AND protect vital interests; different
   rights sit at different points on that spectrum, and a legal order
   need not pick one theory for all of them
                          |
                          v
   HOW THIS FEEDS THE 2025 CORRELATIVITY ANSWER
   INTEREST theory makes the RIGHT -> DUTY direction natural: the
      interest is the REASON for the duty
   WILL theory makes the direction CONTROL-BASED: the holder governs a
      duty that already exists
   EITHER WAY a duty is in the picture -- so the "no necessary
      connection" claim is weakest exactly where CLAIM-RIGHTS are
      concerned, and strongest where LIBERTIES are concerned
```

*Caption: two theories of what a right is for, each unbeatable on its own strong case and each broken on the other's — which is why the mixed position is a conclusion rather than a dodge.*

#### In plain words

Ask what a right is *for* and you get two answers that both sound obviously correct until you put them side by side.

The first says a right exists to give you control. Having a right means you are in charge of somebody else's duty: you can insist on it, or let them off. That is why it fits autonomy so well — the right-holder is a kind of small sovereign over a small patch of other people's conduct.

The second says a right exists to protect something that matters to you. On this view your interest in not being assaulted is what justifies imposing on everyone the duty not to assault you, and the fact that you might never think about it, or might be an infant incapable of thinking about it, changes nothing.

Now the test. A newborn has a right not to be harmed. Who controls the corresponding duty? Not the newborn. Will theory has a real problem. Conversely, an adult can waive a contractual right. If the right exists to protect her interest, why is she allowed to throw the protection away? Interest theory has a real problem. Neither is silly; each is broken by the other's home ground, and that is exactly why most modern systems behave as if rights do both jobs.

#### The doctrine, exactly

✅ **The two theories, stated exactly.** **Will theory** treats rights as **normative control over others' duties**. **Interest theory** treats rights as **protections of significant interests** necessary for well-being.

| Theory | Core idea | Strength | Main difficulty |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Will theory** | rights protect choice, control, agency | strong fit with autonomy and consent | children or severely disabled persons become hard cases |
| **Interest theory** | rights protect important interests necessary for well-being | explains welfare and protective rights well | can underplay the role of active agency |

✅ **The question each asks.** Will theory asks **who controls a duty**; interest theory asks **whose important interest justifies imposing the duty**.

✅ **Why will theory suits liberalism.** It makes the right-holder an agent with normative standing rather than a beneficiary of others' good behaviour, which is exactly what a doctrine of autonomy and consent requires.

✅ **Why interest theory explains what will theory cannot.** It accounts for rights held by those who cannot actively exercise choice in the ordinary sense, including children, and for welfare and protective rights generally.

⚠️ **The mixed position.** Many contemporary views use a **mixed approach**: rights both secure agency and protect vital interests. In practice, many rights systems treat **both agency and welfare** as relevant dimensions, and different rights sit at different points on the spectrum.

⚠️ **The presupposition of the whole dispute.** Rights must be explained through agency, through interests, or through a synthesis of both — the dispute assumes that a right requires *some* underlying account of value, which is precisely what Hohfeld's analytic scheme deliberately withholds.

#### Named evidence you can cite

- **The will/interest dispute** ✅ — will theory makes the right-holder a small sovereign; interest theory makes rights protectors of well-being. ⚠️ Limitation: each theory handles a different hard case badly.
- **Children and the severely disabled** ✅ — the standard hard case that interest theory handles and strict will theory does not.
- **Waiver in contract** ⚠️ — the mirror hard case that will theory handles and strict interest theory does not.
- **Hohfeld** ✅ — the analytic background: the dispute is about the justification of the claim-right whose *form* Hohfeld has already fixed.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING LINE — WRITE OR ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> The will and interest theories are not two definitions competing for the same territory but two answers to the question *what a right is for* — control over another's duty, or protection of an interest that justifies the duty — and each is refuted on precisely the case where the other is strongest.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

**normative control over another's duty** · **protection of a significant interest** · **the right-holder as small sovereign** · **waiver and enforcement** · **the hard case of the infant** · **mixed approach** · **agency and welfare as two dimensions**

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — claim → named evidence → analysis → qualification

> **Claim.** The dispute between will and interest theories is a dispute about the point of a right, and it cannot be settled by definition because each theory is decisively strong on a case where the other collapses. **Named evidence.** Will theory treats a right as normative control over another's duty, so the holder may waive, demand or enforce it, which fits autonomy, consent and contract exactly; interest theory treats a right as the protection of an interest important enough to justify imposing a duty, which is why it accounts naturally for the rights of children and of the severely disabled, who cannot exercise control at all. **Analysis.** The hard cases mirror each other: strict will theory implies that an infant has no rights, which no defensible moral view accepts, while strict interest theory struggles to explain why a competent adult may lawfully waive a protection that exists for her benefit — so the choice is not between a correct and an incorrect theory but between two partial accounts of a plural practice. **Qualification.** The mixed position is therefore a conclusion rather than an evasion: mature legal orders treat agency and welfare as two dimensions along which different rights sit differently, and the residual dispute is not whether both matter but which dimension is basic when they conflict.

#### Objection → reply → residual limit

- **Objection ❓.** The theories compete and cannot be reconciled; a mixed approach is simply an admission that neither works.
- **Reply ⚠️.** In practice, many rights systems treat both agency and welfare as relevant dimensions, and the plurality is explanatory rather than evasive: a system that protects contract, bodily integrity and childhood in the same document is *already* using both accounts, and a theory should describe that practice rather than legislate against it.
- **Residual limit ❓.** The reply is descriptively strong and theoretically incomplete. When agency and interest conflict — paternalist protection against competent refusal is the standard case — the mixed view still owes a criterion for deciding which dimension governs, and it does not supply one.

#### Examiner traps on this session

1. Treating interest theory as "the welfare-state theory". It is a theory of what makes a claim a right, not a policy preference, and it applies equally to the right against assault.
2. Saying will theory "gives more freedom". It gives more *control*; whether that produces more freedom depends on the person's capacity to exercise it, which is precisely the hard case.
3. Presenting the mixed approach without naming the residual conflict. A synthesis that never says what happens when the two dimensions clash is a label, not a position.

#### Mini recap

Will theory: control over another's duty; fits autonomy; breaks on infants. Interest theory: protection of a significant interest; fits welfare and protective rights; breaks on waiver. Mixed approach is defensible but owes a tie-breaking criterion.

---

### SESSION 7 — RIGHTS V: HUMAN RIGHTS — UNIVERSALITY AGAINST CULTURAL RELATIVISM

#### The picture first

```text
   THE 2018 QUESTION, DRAWN AS AN ARGUMENT
   =======================================
   "Human rights and human dignity would no longer be the product of a
    particular culture, rather a COMMON HUMAN ASPIRATION for an ideal
    world."
                          |
        +-----------------+------------------+
        v                                    v
   UNIVERSALIST LINE                    RELATIVIST CHALLENGE
   ------------------                   --------------------
   certain rights belong to ALL         conceptions of personhood,
   human beings simply AS human         community, authority and
```
