##### Why this earns marks

**Answer-writing focus:** "adequately" is empty until a benchmark is supplied. Scripts that praise or dismiss the theory without naming the standard of adequacy produce a verdict with nothing behind it.

**Exam-length execution — 15 marks, about 250 to 330 words:** benchmark two lines; contributions five; shortfall three; objection–reply four; verdict two.

- **Structure:** name the benchmark → contributions → three excluded categories → objection with reply → graded verdict.
- **Doctrine:** contract as a normative test of legitimacy; human rights as universal moral standing.
- **Evidence:** Locke's trust and resistance; Rousseau's general will and self-legislation; the three excluded categories.
- **Distinction:** political legitimacy against universal moral standing.
- **Criticism:** abstraction from unequal starting positions, conceded as residual.

#### Solved PYQ 5 · 2022 · Q4(b) · 15 marks

**Question:** Does idea of unconditional rights necessarily lead to anarchy? Critically examine.

##### Demand decoding

| Element | Requirement |
|---|---|
| Directive | "Critically examine" — the objection–reply layer *is* the answer, and the modal word must be attacked |
| Core issue | Whether unlimited rights produce disorder, or destroy themselves before disorder arises |
| Scope boundary | Fifteen marks: the collision argument, the four boundary principles, the erosion counter-worry |
| Key distinction | Limitation against nullification; self-defeat against anarchy |
| Non-negotiables | Both operative words defined; the Hohfeldian refinement; the closing on accountability |

##### Independent model answer

**Fixing the two words.** ⚠️ "Unconditional" means admitting **no limit whatever**, not merely "strong". "Necessarily" is a **modal** claim, so it is defeated if the consequence does not follow of conceptual necessity.

**The collision argument.** ✅ Rights are claims that plural persons must be able to hold **simultaneously**. If every asserted liberty were absolute, collisions would be unavoidable — and unresolvable, because any procedure for resolving them would itself constitute a condition. ⚠️ Hohfeld sharpens this: two unconditional **liberties** conflict in fact, while two unconditional **claim-rights** conflict in normative structure, since each entails a duty in the other party that the other's right forbids.

**What follows.** ✅ Rights therefore require **boundary principles**: others' equal rights, harm constraints, public order, and institutional adjudication. This is not denial of rights but the **condition of their coexistence**, since liberty is plural and socially situated.

**The decisive distinction.** ✅ **Limiting** rights is not the same as **nullifying** them. The problem is arbitrary restriction, not principled coordination — and a principled limit is reasoned, bounded and reviewable, while an arbitrary one is none of these.

**Objection and reply.** ❓ Once limits are admitted, the state may steadily erode rights, each restriction plausible alone and the aggregate destructive. ⚠️ Hence the answer closes on **accountability**: every restriction must itself be justified, proportionate and judicially reviewable, so that the limits are limited in turn. ❓ The residual vulnerability is empirical — where review is captured or unavailable to the affected group, the erosion objection returns in full.

**Verdict.** ⚠️ Unconditional rights are **self-defeating rather than necessarily anarchy-producing**: they destroy the coexistence that makes rights exercisable, so the failure is internal to the concept and does not depend on any prediction of social breakdown.

##### Why this earns marks

**Answer-writing focus:** the modal word must be attacked directly. A script that describes disorder has offered a sociological forecast where the stem asked for a conceptual necessity claim.

**Exam-length execution — 15 marks, about 250 to 330 words:** definitions two lines; collision four; boundary principles two; limitation/nullification two; objection–reply four; verdict two.

- **Structure:** define both operative words → collision argument → boundary principles → limitation against nullification → erosion objection with reply → verdict.
- **Doctrine:** rights as simultaneously holdable claims; principled limitation as a condition of coexistence.
- **Evidence:** the four boundary principles; the Hohfeldian refinement of the collision.
- **Distinction:** self-defeat against anarchy; principled coordination against arbitrary restriction.
- **Criticism:** the empirical vulnerability of review, conceded as residual.

#### Solved PYQ 6 · 2023 · Q2(b) · 15 marks

**Question:** Do you agree that duty and accountability must be given priority over rights for the better functioning of a State? Justify your answer.

##### Demand decoding

| Element | Requirement |
|---|---|
| Directive | "Do you agree … Justify" — a position plus a named criterion, not a survey |
| Core issue | Whether a priority ranking among the three printed terms can be defended |
| Scope boundary | Fifteen marks: criterion first, then the grid, then the role of accountability, then the verdict |
| Key distinction | Ethical priority in motivation against political priority in protection |
| Non-negotiables | The three-position grid with merits and risks; accountability as the term that saves duty-priority |

##### Independent model answer

**Naming the criterion.** ⚠️ A priority claim is empty until a standard is fixed. Two standards are available: **ethical priority**, which asks what best motivates good conduct, and **political priority**, which asks what best protects persons against abuse. The stem's phrase "better functioning of a State" points to the first while presupposing the second.

**The three positions.** ✅ **Rights-first** protects the person against coercion and shields minorities and dissent, at the risk of an atomised entitlement culture. ✅ **Duty-first** preserves order and reciprocity and stresses social responsibility, at the risk of paternalism or conformity. ⚠️ The **balanced view** treats rights and duties as mutually sustaining and fits constitutional democracy best, but requires institutional vigilance.

**Partial agreement.** ⚠️ The stem is right that a republic cannot function on claims alone. Duties turn abstract norms into expectations of conduct, and democratic citizenship depends on tolerance, honesty, restraint and respect for disagreement — habits no legal order can codify or manufacture. ✅ Gandhi's duty-centrism states the strongest version: rights become secure when persons discipline themselves.

**Why the priority cannot be unqualified.** ❓ Duty-language is dangerous when it binds downward only, and it has historically been invoked by authority to silence dissent. ✅ This is exactly why the stem's inclusion of **accountability** matters: if public power must answer, be judged and be corrected, then asking citizens for duty is not a demand for submission. Accountability prevents both state excess and civic irresponsibility.

**Verdict.** ⚠️ Duty may have **ethical priority** in motivating conduct, but rights retain **political priority** in protecting persons against abuse; accountability is the condition under which the first is safe. ⚠️ I therefore agree in part: duty and accountability deserve emphasis, not precedence, since no stable democratic order can rest on only one of these values.

##### Why this earns marks

**Answer-writing focus:** ranking without a criterion is assertion, and "balance" without a criterion is a longer assertion. Name the standard in the first three lines and the rest of the answer writes itself.

**Exam-length execution — 15 marks, about 250 to 330 words:** criterion three lines; grid four; partial agreement four; qualification four; verdict two.

- **Structure:** name the criterion → three-position grid → concede the stem's strength → name the danger → graded verdict.
- **Doctrine:** duty as a discipline of coexistence; accountability as the ethics of public power.
- **Evidence:** the merits-and-risks grid; the civic habits law cannot manufacture; Gandhi's duty-centrism.
- **Distinction:** ethical priority against political priority; emphasis against precedence.
- **Criticism:** duty-talk used downward only, met by accountability rather than dismissed.

#### Solved PYQ 7 · 2024 · Q1(b) · 10 marks

**Question:** Present a brief account of origin and development of Social Contract Theory.

##### Demand decoding

| Element | Requirement |
|---|---|
| Directive | "Origin and development" — chronology **plus** conceptual movement, never three biographies |
| Core issue | What each successor **changes**, and why the change matters |
| Scope boundary | Ten marks: one line on origin, then the three positions as changes, then a one-line verdict |
| Key distinction | Transfer, trust and general will |
| Non-negotiables | Hobbes irreversible; Locke's trust and resistance; Rousseau's relocation of sovereignty |

##### Independent model answer

**Origin.** ✅ Social contract theory explains the origin and justification of the state through an agreement, actual or hypothetical, among individuals. Its founding achievement is to derive political obligation from the **standpoint of individuals** rather than from divine right or conquest.

**Hobbes.** ✅ From a state of nature marked by insecurity, fear and conflict, the contract secures **peace** through a broad and irreversible transfer of judgment to a strong sovereign. Rights are surrendered extensively for the sake of self-preservation.

**Locke — the first development.** ✅ The state of nature becomes more norm-governed, containing inconveniences rather than war. The arrangement becomes a **fiduciary trust** for the impartial protection of rights already held, so government is limited, natural rights are retained, and violation of the trust justifies **resistance**.

**Rousseau — the second development.** ✅ Freedom in the state of nature has been corrupted by inequality, and the contract's purpose becomes **collective self-rule**. Sovereignty stays with the people as the **general will**, so obedience becomes self-legislation and civil freedom is moral autonomy.

**The fiction objection, answered.** ❓ No actual historical contract occurred. ✅ The theory's value is **normative rather than historical**: it asks what terms of rule free and equal persons could reasonably accept.

**Verdict.** ⚠️ The development is a sequence of deliberate changes — transfer, then trust and resistance, then relocation of sovereignty — and it is this movement, not the shared vocabulary, that constitutes the theory's history.

##### Why this earns marks

**Answer-writing focus:** at ten marks the temptation is three summaries. The stem's word is *development*, and marks follow the changes: what Locke adds to Hobbes, and what Rousseau relocates.

**Exam-length execution — 10 marks, about 150 to 220 words:** origin two lines; Hobbes two; Locke three; Rousseau three; fiction objection one; verdict one.

- **Structure:** origin → Hobbes → Locke's change → Rousseau's change → fiction objection → movement verdict.
- **Doctrine:** obligation derived from the individual's standpoint.
- **Evidence:** irreversible transfer; fiduciary trust with a right of resistance; the general will.
- **Distinction:** transfer against trust against relocation of sovereignty.
- **Criticism:** the historical-fiction objection, answered normatively in a single line.

#### Solved PYQ 8 · 2024 · Q3(a) · 20 marks

**Question:** Do you agree with the view that Aristotle was more successful than Plato in steering a middle course between 'Statism' and 'individualism'? Discuss with arguments.

##### Demand decoding

| Element | Requirement |
|---|---|
| Directive | "Do you agree … Discuss with arguments" — agreement must be granted and immediately qualified |
| Core issue | Whether an intermediate sphere between person and polis survives in each thinker |
| Scope boundary | Twenty marks: run the axes, then the shared perfectionism, then the exclusions |
| Key distinction | Comparative success against modern individualism |
| Non-negotiables | Excessive unity; household and property; differentiated constitution; the exclusions |

##### Independent model answer

**Thesis.** ⚠️ I agree, but only comparatively: Aristotle steers the better middle because an **intermediate sphere** survives in his polity, while both thinkers remain perfectionists and Aristotle's exclusions block any modern endorsement.

**Fixing the axis.** ⚠️ A middle course between statism and individualism is measured by whether anything stands **between** the person and the political whole — household, property, association, plural constitutional roles. Neither thinker is an atomistic individualist, so the comparison must be conducted on that axis.

**Plato.** ✅ The *Republic* makes justice **functional harmony** under philosopher-rule: each part of the city performs its proper work. To prevent faction, the guardian class gives up **private property and the private family**. ⚠️ This is a deliberate subordination of the private domain to political unity, and the person's good is specified through proper function in the ordered city — which places Plato nearer the statist pole.

**Aristotle.** ✅ The polis is **natural** and exists for the good life, but Aristotle criticises **excessive unity** directly: a polis is a **plurality**, not one household or one person. ✅ Household and private property therefore remain, though their use should serve common purposes, and citizenship is **activity within a differentiated constitution** rather than the discharge of an assigned function. ⚠️ Person and polis are thus related rather than the person being absorbed.

**The comparative judgment.** ⚠️ Aristotle preserves more social plurality, better protects the intermediate sphere, and relates rather than absorbs the individual good. That is exactly what steering a middle course requires, and the stem's comparison is therefore sound.

**The qualification.** ❓ Both remain **perfectionists**: neither treats the state as neutral among conceptions of the good. ✅ And Aristotle's polity rests on **slavery, the exclusion of women and restricted citizenship**, which are incompatible with modern equality. His intermediate sphere is available only to a restricted citizen body.

**Verdict.** ⚠️ Aristotle's success is **comparative, not liberal**. He defeats Plato's excessive unity without becoming an individualist, and an unqualified endorsement would misdescribe both thinkers.

##### Why this earns marks

**Answer-writing focus:** the marks are split between agreeing and qualifying. A script that only agrees turns Aristotle into a modern liberal; a script that only qualifies never answers the comparison the stem sets.

**Exam-length execution — 20 marks, about 330 to 400 words:** thesis two lines; axis two; Plato four; Aristotle five; comparative judgment three; qualification three; verdict two.

- **Structure:** thesis → fix the axis → Plato → Aristotle → comparative judgment → shared perfectionism and exclusions → verdict.
- **Doctrine:** the intermediate sphere as the test of a middle course.
- **Evidence:** guardian property and family arrangements; the plurality argument; the differentiated constitution.
- **Distinction:** comparative middle against modern individualism.
- **Criticism:** perfectionism and the exclusions, which limit the endorsement without defeating the comparison.

#### Solved PYQ 9 · 2025 · Q4(c) · 15 marks

**Question:** "Duties are of the nature of obligation while Rights are of the nature of entitlement. Therefore there is no necessary connection between the two." Do you agree with this statement? Give reasons and justification for your answer.

##### Demand decoding

| Element | Requirement |
|---|---|
| Directive | "Do you agree … Give reasons" — the stem is an *inference*, so premise and conclusion must be assessed separately |
| Core issue | Whether analytic distinguishability entails absence of necessary connection |
| Scope boundary | Fifteen marks: concede the premise, refuse the inference, split by Hohfeldian incident |
| Key distinction | Logical strictness against normative necessity |
| Non-negotiables | The four incidents with correlatives; the empty-entitlement and authoritarian consequences |

##### Independent model answer

**Position.** ⚠️ I agree with the statement's **premise** and reject its **inference**. Obligation and entitlement are genuinely distinct concepts; but analytic distinguishability does not entail the absence of a necessary connection.

**Conceding the premise.** ✅ Duties are obligations to do, avoid, respect or support; rights are justified claims, liberties, powers or immunities. The two are **analytically distinguishable**, which is why we have two words rather than one.

**Refusing the inference — the Hohfeldian split.** ✅ "Right" names four juridical positions, each with its own correlative: a **claim-right** correlates with a **duty**; a **liberty** correlates only with a **no-right** in others; a **power** correlates with **liability**; an **immunity** correlates with **disability**. ⚠️ Therefore correlativity is **strict** for claim-rights — my right against assault simply *is* your duty not to assault me — and **absent** for liberties, since a liberty imposes no positive duty of assistance. The connection is conceptual for one class of rights and institutional for the rest.

**Why the connection is not dispensable.** ✅ Rights are **socially actionable**, not isolated metaphysical ornaments. A legal right to remedy implies institutions' duty to hear claims. ⚠️ Two consequences follow: entitlements without correlative obligations remain **empty**, and obligations without any corresponding claims risk **authoritarianism**. The concepts are distinguishable but **not socially separable**.

**The strongest point on the other side.** ❓ For liberties the correlativity thesis really is false as stated, so a candidate who claims that *every* right entails a duty has overreached. ⚠️ The honest position concedes this rather than defending an untenable universal.

**Verdict.** ⚠️ Not agreed as a whole: the premise is sound, the inference invalid. **Logical strictness** does not hold for every use of "right", but **normative necessity** does — a right-worthy order requires duties somewhere in the background, and the two are inseparable in practice even where they are separable in analysis.

##### Why this earns marks

**Answer-writing focus:** the mark-bearing move is the **correlativity analysis**. Showing which rights have strict correlative duties and which do not defeats the inference precisely, where "two sides of a coin" merely asserts the universality the liberty case refutes.

**Exam-length execution — 15 marks, about 250 to 330 words:** position two lines; premise conceded two; Hohfeldian split six; consequences three; concession two; verdict three.

- **Structure:** position → concede premise → four incidents and their correlatives → empty/authoritarian consequences → concede the liberty case → modal verdict.
- **Doctrine:** correlativity, distinguished into strict and institutional forms.
- **Evidence:** the four Hohfeldian incidents; the right against assault; the right to remedy.
- **Distinction:** logical strictness against normative necessity; distinguishable against separable.
- **Criticism:** the liberty counter-example, conceded rather than concealed.

### ORIGINAL MAINS PRACTICE WITH MODEL SOLUTIONS

> ⚠️ These six questions are **original practice**, written in the style of the paper but not drawn from any official examination. Word bands are the same as above.

#### Original 1 · 10 marks

**Question:** Explain the distinction between a claim-right and a liberty, and show why the distinction matters for the relation between the individual and the state. Answer in about 150 words.

##### Independent model answer

**The distinction.** ✅ A **claim-right** is a justified claim whose correlative is a **duty** in a determinate other: my right against assault is your duty not to assault me. A **liberty** or privilege is a freedom to act whose correlative is merely a **no-right** in others: nobody may demand that I desist, but nobody is bound to assist me either.

**Why it matters.** ⚠️ First, it disciplines rights-talk. Claims of the form "I have a right to X" are inflated whenever no bearer of a corresponding duty can be named. ✅ Second, it fixes what the state owes: liberties require **forbearance**, while claim-rights may require **provision, protection and remedy**, which is a far heavier institutional demand.

**Verdict.** ⚠️ The distinction therefore separates what the state must merely not do from what it must actually do — and confusing the two produces either hollow entitlements or an unbounded expectation of provision.

##### Why this earns marks

- **Structure:** definition of each incident with its correlative → two consequences → verdict on state obligation.
- **Doctrine:** Hohfeld's incidents deployed rather than merely listed.
- **Evidence:** the assault example for the claim-right; the absence of an assistance duty for the liberty.
- **Distinction:** forbearance against provision.
- **Criticism:** the inflation risk, named as the practical cost of the confusion.

#### Original 2 · 10 marks

**Question:** What is meant by accountability, and why is it insufficient to identify it with periodic elections? Answer in about 150 words.

##### Independent model answer

**The concept.** ✅ Accountability means that those who exercise public power must **explain, justify and bear consequences** for their decisions. It is the bridge from authority to legitimacy: power without answerability is domination, while accountable power can be defended as public trust.

**The three dimensions.** ✅ **Answerability** requires information and public justification; **enforceability** requires review, remedy, sanction or removal where standards were breached; **responsiveness** requires changed conduct, repaired harm and institutional learning. ⚠️ Each fails when isolated — explanation without correction becomes ritual, punishment without reason-giving becomes arbitrary, and reactive correction without prospective rules leaves the next case exposed.

**Why elections are insufficient.** ⚠️ An election is periodic, aggregate and retrospective; it cannot examine a particular decision, supply a remedy to a particular claimant, or require an institution to learn. ✅ Political accountability itself extends beyond voting to opposition, deliberation, publicity and criticism, and legal and moral answerability are distinct arenas again.

##### Why this earns marks

- **Structure:** definition → three dimensions with failure modes → the specific inadequacy of elections.
- **Doctrine:** accountability as the authority-to-legitimacy bridge.
- **Evidence:** the three dimensions; the extension of political accountability beyond voting.
- **Distinction:** periodic and aggregate against particular and remedial.
- **Criticism:** the ritual, arbitrariness and non-learning failure modes, named precisely.

#### Original 3 · 15 marks

**Question:** "The state may remove hindrances to freedom, but it may not define what freedom is for." Critically examine this claim with reference to the idealist and welfare-liberal accounts of the individual–state relation. Answer in about 250 words.

##### Independent model answer

**The claim's structure.** ⚠️ The statement concedes an **enabling** role to the state and denies it a **defining** one. Its plausibility depends on whether that line can be held.

**Where it is strongest.** ✅ T. H. Green's idealism supports it directly: the state is a **means to the common good**, and its proper work is to **remove remediable hindrances to self-realisation** while leaving moral agency with citizens. ✅ Welfare liberalism supports it too: formal rights are hollow where poverty, illness, ignorance or dependency makes choice unusable, so a social minimum and fair opportunity are justified without licensing direction of life. ⚠️ On both accounts positive freedom is **effective capacity**, not an authorisation to prescribe ends.

**Where it comes under pressure.** ❓ Hegel's account makes freedom **concrete** through participation in rational institutions, which suggests that institutions partly constitute what freedom is rather than merely clearing obstacles. ❓ And every hindrance-removal presupposes a judgment about which obstacles matter — a judgment that is not itself content-free.

**Objection and reply.** ❓ Once the state defines the rational or higher self, coercion may be presented as liberation. ⚠️ The defensible reconstruction keeps enabling action **rights-bound, decentralised where possible and open to public challenge**; provision may still become paternalistic or dependency-producing, so the reply is a discipline rather than a guarantee.

**Verdict.** ⚠️ The claim survives as a **regulative principle** rather than a sharp boundary: the state cannot avoid some conception of what hindrances are, but it can be held to justifying that conception publicly and to leaving the exercise of agency with the citizen.

##### Why this earns marks

- **Structure:** analyse the claim → strongest support → pressure points → objection with reply → regulative verdict.
- **Doctrine:** Green's hindrance-removal; welfare liberalism's hollow-rights insight; Hegel's concrete freedom.
- **Evidence:** conditional political obligation; the social minimum; the paternalism and dependency limits.
- **Distinction:** enabling against defining; regulative principle against sharp boundary.
- **Criticism:** the non-neutrality of hindrance-identification, conceded rather than evaded.

#### Original 4 · 15 marks

**Question:** Distinguish civil disobedience from conscientious objection, resistance and revolution, and examine whether a constitutional democracy can consistently accommodate any of them. Answer in about 250 words.

##### Independent model answer

**The four rungs.** ✅ **Conscientious objection** aims to avoid personal complicity, may be private, seeks exemption and typically accepts the penalty, and accepts the system's legitimacy. ✅ **Civil disobedience** aims to persuade the majority, is **essentially public**, breaches a specific law with **acceptance of legal consequences**, and appeals to the system's own principles. ✅ **Resistance** aims to obstruct a specific grave injustice, may be covert, may refuse the penalty, and denies the legitimacy of the acts obstructed. ✅ **Revolution** aims to replace the constitutional order, is organised and often armed, and denies the system's legitimacy wholesale.

**The two defining features of civil disobedience.** ⚠️ Publicity with appeal to shared principles, and acceptance of the legal penalty — the second being what demonstrates that the actor contests **one law** rather than repudiating law as such. ✅ Non-violence is standardly added; on the Gandhian account it is **constitutive**, since means and ends are inseparable.

**Can a constitutional democracy accommodate them?** ⚠️ It can accommodate the first two without contradiction, because both accept the order's legitimacy and neither claims a legal right to disobey. ❌ No legal order can **recognise a right** to civil disobedience, since acceptance of the penalty is part of the concept. ✅ In India, Articles 19(1)(a) and 19(1)(b) protect protest subject to reasonable restrictions; they do not protect law-breaking, and Article 51A is non-justiciable.

**Verdict.** ⚠️ Accommodation is moral and political rather than legal: the order tolerates and learns from penalty-accepting dissent, while resistance and revolution answer to progressively heavier burdens of justification. ❓ Ambedkar's objection stands wherever constitutional channels are genuinely open to the group in question.

##### Why this earns marks

- **Structure:** four rungs on five criteria → the two defining features → the accommodation question → verdict with the standing objection.
- **Doctrine:** the graded ladder; publicity and penalty-acceptance as constitutive.
- **Evidence:** Articles 19(1)(a), 19(1)(b) and 51A; Ambedkar's availability condition.
- **Distinction:** moral and political accommodation against legal recognition.
- **Criticism:** the availability condition, left contestable rather than declared settled.

#### Original 5 · 20 marks

**Question:** "Political obligation, if it exists at all, is neither general nor content-independent." Critically examine this claim, and assess whether its acceptance would weaken the authority of law. Answer in about 330 words.

##### Independent model answer

**Fixing the terms.** ✅ **Political obligation** is a moral duty to obey the law of one's own state **because it is the law** — not fear of sanction, not agreement with the law's content, and not the merely jurisprudential fact of legal validity. ⚠️ The claim under examination denies two of the four standard tests: **generality** and **content-independence**.

**Testing the candidate grounds.** ✅ **Consent** founders because almost nobody has expressly consented, and tacit consent inferred from residence or use of roads is not voluntary in any meaningful sense. ✅ **Fair play** — the family examiners most often reward — reconstructs as: political society is a cooperative scheme; its benefits exist because most bear burdens; I have enjoyed them; to take the benefit while refusing the burden is free-riding; free-riding is unfair; so I owe compliance. ⚠️ It fails because public order is **non-excludable**: an unsolicited benefit I could not decline generates no debt, and the repaired version — restricted to knowingly and willingly accepted benefits — binds precisely those who least need binding. ✅ The **natural duty of justice** fails **particularity**, binding me to any just state. ⚠️ **Associative** accounts risk deriving obligation from mere membership however unjust the association, and **gratitude** can be claimed by an oppressive benefactor.

**The sceptical conclusion, stated exactly.** ✅ A. John Simmons is the canonical modern sceptic: the standard grounds fail to bind **all and only** the members of a particular state. ⚠️ But the conclusion is narrower than it sounds — it denies a **general, content-independent** duty while leaving most laws bindingly supported by independent moral reasons against harm, fraud and unfairness.

**The strongest reply.** ✅ Joseph Raz's **service conception** relocates the question: an authority is legitimate when its directives help subjects **conform better to reasons that already apply to them**, and those directives then function as **pre-emptive** reasons replacing rather than outweighing private calculation. ⚠️ Authority is thereby bounded by its function rather than unlimited.

**Verdict.** ⚠️ The claim is largely sustained and its consequences are modest. ❓ Law's authority is weakened only if authority requires unconditional obedience; on the service conception and on the disobedience ladder, defeasible obligation is compatible with a stable legal order, because publicity, non-violence, exhaustion of remedies and acceptance of penalty are severe filters.

##### Why this earns marks

- **Structure:** fix the terms → run the four tests across the families → state the sceptical conclusion narrowly → Raz's reply → consequences verdict.
- **Doctrine:** generality, particularity, content-independence, defeasibility; the service conception.
- **Evidence:** the fair-play reconstruction and its non-excludability defeat; the particularity failure of the natural duty.
- **Distinction:** being obliged against having an obligation; general duty against particular binding reasons.
- **Criticism:** the residual that Raz's justification is instrumental, so authority is bounded rather than absolute.

#### Original 6 · 20 marks

**Question:** "Rights protect individuality, duties sustain social order, and accountability legitimises coercion; but each becomes destructive without the other two." Discuss this claim as an account of the relation between the individual and the state. Answer in about 330 words.

##### Independent model answer

**Thesis.** ⚠️ The claim is the strongest available summary of this relation, because it identifies not three virtues but three **directions** of a single relation, each of which degenerates in a determinate way when isolated.

**Rights without duties and accountability.** ✅ A right is a justified claim, liberty, power or immunity; but rights are **socially actionable**, and a claim-right is meaningful only where a determinate other bears the correlative duty. ⚠️ Detached from limits and reciprocity, rights become **licence**: unconditional claims collide unresolvably, because any resolving procedure would itself be a condition. ✅ Detached from accountability, they lack remedy, since a right no forum will vindicate is a right in name.

**Duties without rights and accountability.** ✅ Duties turn abstract norms into expectations of conduct and make coexistence possible, and democratic citizenship depends on habits — tolerance, honesty, restraint — that no legal order can manufacture. ❓ But duty is regularly invoked by authority to silence dissent. ⚠️ Detached from rights, duty becomes **subordination**; and this is precisely why the safeguard is accountability, which makes the demand for duty reciprocal rather than downward. ✅ Ambedkar's objection is the sharpest instance: a duty-first order in a stratified society can be captured by dominant groups.

**Accountability without rights and duties.** ✅ Accountability requires answerability, enforceability and responsiveness together. ⚠️ Without rights it has no **standard** by which to judge, so review becomes procedural ritual; without duties it lacks the civic sustenance that keeps reviewing institutions functioning. ⚠️ Power without answerability is **domination** however efficient it is, since a state may be efficient yet unaccountable.

**The strongest objection.** ❓ It may be said that this three-way balance is a description rather than a ranking, and that real cases force a choice. ⚠️ The reply is that a criterion is available: duty holds **ethical priority** in motivating conduct, while rights hold **political priority** in protecting persons against abuse, with accountability as the condition under which the first is safe.

**Verdict.** ⚠️ The claim is sound as a structural thesis and incomplete as a decision procedure. ✅ Its enduring value is that it makes the individual neither an atom outside society nor an expendable part of the state, and specifies a justified relation between dignity and authority.

##### Why this earns marks

- **Structure:** thesis → three isolation-failures in turn → strongest objection with reply → structural verdict.
- **Doctrine:** the three directions of one relation; the three dimensions of accountability.
- **Evidence:** the collision argument; the civic habits law cannot manufacture; Ambedkar's capture objection.
- **Distinction:** licence, subordination and domination as three determinate degenerations.
- **Criticism:** the balance is structural rather than decisional, met by naming a priority criterion.
## OPTIONAL ADVANCED DEPTH — NOT REQUIRED FOR A CORE ANSWER

> **Fence.** Everything above this line is answer-complete for all nine owned previous-year parts. This block exists for **one** added debate, **one** sharper objection with its reply, or **one** controlled comparison inside a 15- or 20-marker. It is enrichment, never a prerequisite. ⚠️ Do not sink time into highly technical analytic jurisprudence once Hohfeld and the main rights theories are secure, and do not let the topic drift into generic civics.

### A1. The residue of political obligation after promotion ✅

The core prerequisite for this block is the whole Basic session: rights, duties and accountability; natural, legal and moral rights; Hohfeld; will against interest theory at a basic level; Gandhi and the Indian tradition; the social contract; and the 2018–2025 demands on human rights, accountability, unconditional rights, contract, statism–individualism and the rights–duties relation.

- **What was promoted ✅.** Political obligation in all its families, civil disobedience and Hohfeld's incidents **fired and were promoted to Core on 15 August 2026**. They are examinable Core, not optional extras, and Session 20 teaches them in full.
- **The optional residue ⚠️.** Two questions remain second-order: **philosophical anarchism's denial** that any general obligation exists at all, and the **fair-play against consent priority dispute** — which family should be treated as basic when both are available.
- **The one place it earns marks ⚠️.** In a 20-marker on obedience, naming the priority dispute shows that the six families are not a list but a competition, and that a candidate who chooses fair play over consent is making a defended choice rather than reciting an inventory.

### A2. Rights as choice against rights as protection ⚠️

- **The contending positions ⚠️.** Will theory ties rights to **agency and control** over another's duty; interest theory ties rights to the **protection of important interests**.
- **Why it stays optional ✅.** It is helpful for sharpening the 2025 rights–duties question, but it is not indispensable: the Hohfeldian split already defeats the stem's inference without it.
- **The one place it earns marks ⚠️.** Where a stem concerns the rights of children, the severely disabled or future generations, the will/interest dispute supplies the reason the case is hard, instead of leaving the difficulty unexplained.

### A3. Philosophical anarchism as a standing position ❓

- **The contending positions ❓.** Legitimate authority is **deeply suspect** and no general obligation has been demonstrated; the rival view is that coordination, security and justice **require justified political rule**.
- **Why it stays optional ✅.** The owner already covers the anarchist challenge at exam-safe level in Session 17, and the sceptical position appears as the sixth family in Session 20.
- **The boundary ⚠️.** Anarchism and Marxism as **full ideologies** belong to **Political Ideologies**; here they enter only as relation-models bearing on rights, duties and accountability.

### A4. Accountability as answerability against accountability as sanction ⚠️

- **The contending positions ⚠️.** One view emphasises **giving reasons publicly**; another adds **enforceable consequences** as essential rather than supplementary.
- **Why it stays optional ✅.** It is useful in state-ethics answers, but it is more precise than the corpus demands directly, and the Basic block's three-dimension analysis already contains both halves.
- **The one place it earns marks ⚠️.** In a 20-marker on the 2019 or 2023 stems, it sharpens the claim that explanation without correction becomes ritual — by showing that the dispute is about which dimension is **constitutive** rather than merely desirable.

### A5. The strongest additional objection and reply ⚠️

- **Objection.** **Associative or duty-first accounts can legitimise inherited hierarchy by calling obedience "belonging".** Membership becomes the ground, and the association's own injustice becomes invisible to the theory that appeals to it.
- **Reply.** Duties are philosophically defensible **only when the association itself is consistent with equal dignity and public justification**; otherwise duty-talk becomes ideological cover for domination.
- **Why this is the best available upgrade ⚠️.** It converts the residual limit of Sessions 11 and 20 from a concession into an argument, and it is the single most reusable sentence in this block — usable on the 2020 tradition stem, the 2023 priority stem and any obedience question.

### A6. Safe Indian deployment in a 15- or 20-marker ⚠️

Use the tension between **rights claims, civic duties, protest, welfare entitlements and public accountability** in a constitutional democracy. The safe move is conceptual: show why **rights without institutions are fragile** and why **duties without rights become paternalism**. Keep every caution from Sessions 11 and 20 — Fundamental Duties are non-justiciable, Articles 19(1)(a) and 19(1)(b) protect protest and not law-breaking, legal permission is not moral justification, and no Indian statute or judgment recognises a right to civil disobedience. ❌ Make no empirical claim about any Indian government, party or period.

### A7. Promotion trigger and source trail ✅

- **Already fired ✅.** Political obligation in all four principal families, civil disobedience and Hohfeld's incidents were promoted on 15 August 2026 and are Core.
- **Promote next ⚠️** only if a previous-year part asks directly about the **answerability/sanction distinction** or the **will/interest theory of rights**.
- **Credible source trail ✅.** Locke, *Two Treatises of Government*; Hobbes, *Leviathan*; Rousseau, *The Social Contract*; Hohfeld, *Fundamental Legal Conceptions*; Hart, "Are There Any Natural Rights?"; Joseph Raz, *The Morality of Freedom*; A. John Simmons, *Moral Principles and Political Obligations*. Cited by author and title only, with no page, chapter, edition or invented quotation.

## CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

> Compressed recall for the whole clause. Not a second reading of the teaching: definitions, spines, matrices, traps, routes and closing lines only. Parts A–L carry the content; part M carries the complete ASCII master flow.

### A. Say it exactly — the definition bank

| Term | The sentence to write |
|---|---|
| **Right** | A justified claim, liberty, power or immunity recognised within a moral or legal order — a **protected normative position**, not a preference ✅ |
| **Duty** | An obligation to do, avoid, respect or support something owed to others, institutions, community or oneself ✅ |
| **Accountability** | Those who exercise public power must **explain, justify and bear consequences** for their decisions ✅ |
| **Individual** | Bearer of agency, dignity, interests and moral status ✅ |
| **State** | Organised public authority claiming binding power ✅ |
| **Mechanistic theory** | The state is a means to prior individual ends; persons are logically prior ✅ |
| **Organic theory** | The political whole is partly constitutive of the person's good ✅ |
| **Political obligation** | A moral duty to obey the law of one's own state **because it is the law** ✅ |
| **Civil disobedience** | Public, non-violent breach of a specific law with **acceptance of the legal penalty** ✅ |

**Statement bank — crisp openers.** ✅ Rights are protections of personhood; duties are disciplines of coexistence; accountability is the ethics of public power. ✅ A democratic state is justified not by mere command but by rights-protection and answerable authority. ⚠️ The rights–duties relation is analytically distinguishable but normatively inseparable. ⚠️ Gandhi's duty-centrism is not anti-rights; it is a critique of rights without moral self-restraint. ⚠️ Human-rights universalism is strongest when defended as common human aspiration without denying cultural plurality. ✅ Social contract theory secularises the origin of the state by grounding political obligation in the standpoint of individuals. ⚠️ The individual is neither an atom outside society nor an expendable part of the state.

### B. The three-direction spine — open every answer with this

| Direction | What it asserts | Degeneration if isolated | Owned stems it serves |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Rights — claim against** ✅ | protected interests, choices and agency | **licence** | 2018, 2020, 2021, 2022, 2025 |
| **Duties — obligation through** ✅ | respect, restraint, support, lawful conduct | **subordination** | 2023, 2025 |
| **Accountability — power answers** ✅ | explanation, judgment, correction | **domination** | 2019, 2023 |

**The prior question ⚠️:** *why obey at all?* — political obligation. **The consequent question ⚠️:** *what may I do when the answer fails?* — the disobedience ladder.

### C. Rights — the six grounds and the four incidents

| Ground | The claim | Named anchor |
|---|---|---|
| **Nature** ✅ | rights precede the state and constrain it | Locke |
| **Law** ✅ | a right exists where law supplies claimant, duty-bearer, remedy | Bentham |
| **Morality** ✅ | rights justified by ethical reasoning before enactment | the anti-slavery and anti-colonial claims |
| **Will** ✅ | rights are normative control over another's duty | will theory |
| **Interest** ✅ | rights protect interests important to well-being | interest theory |
| **Dignity** ✅ | rights belong to all human beings as human | human-rights doctrine |

| Incident | Correlative | Opposite |
|---|---|---|
| **Claim-right** ✅ | duty | no-right |
| **Liberty / privilege** ✅ | no-right | duty |
| **Power** ✅ | liability | disability |
| **Immunity** ✅ | disability | liability |

⚠️ **The one line that answers 2025:** correlativity is **strict for claim-rights** and **absent for liberties**, so the connection is conceptual for one class and institutional for the rest.

### D. The six relation-models — locate the case before grading it

| Model | Image of the relation | Freedom is… | Main danger |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Mechanistic** ✅ | instrument created by prior persons | a protected sphere | atomism; hollow formal freedom |
| **Organic** ✅ | whole partly constitutes the person's good | participation in rational institutions | statism; silenced conscience |
| **Welfare / positive liberal** ✅ | instrumental, but removes hindrances | effective capacity | paternalism; dependence |
| **Marxist** ✅/⚠️ | class state universalises a partial interest | emancipation from material domination | under-protected civil liberty in transition |
| **Anarchist** ✅/⚠️ | coercion has no proven right to rule | autonomous voluntary cooperation | coordination, adjudication, security deficits |
| **Communitarian** ✅/⚠️ | the self is socially embedded | situated autonomy | conformity; silenced internal minorities |

⚠️ **Qualified synthesis:** constitutional democracy is mechanistic in insisting institutions serve persons, organic in recognising that citizenship is socially formed.

### E. Duties — sources, kinds and the Indian idiom

- ✅ **Definition:** obligation to do, avoid, respect or support, owed to others, institutions, community or oneself.
- ✅ **Kinds:** legal · moral · civic · political · professional · spiritual. ❌ Do not reduce duty to legal duty.
- ✅ **Legal duty:** basis in law and procedure; enforced by courts, penalties, sanction.
- ✅ **Moral duty:** basis in conscience, ethical reason, social norm; enforced by praise, blame, self-judgment, public opinion.
- ✅ **The controlling argument:** law cannot codify the whole of ethical life, so democratic citizenship depends on **tolerance, honesty, restraint, respect for disagreement and concern for public good** — habits law cannot manufacture.
- ⚠️ **Dharma:** obligation to family, society, living beings, truth and order; the person embedded in relations; at its strongest **responsibility, restraint and moral vocation**, not obedience; must be filtered against sacralised hierarchy.
- ✅ **Gandhi:** rights grow out of well-performed duties; duty educates the moral self; *swaraj* is ethical self-rule; neither anti-state nor centralising statist.
- ✅ **Article 51A:** inserted by the Constitution (**Forty-second Amendment**) Act, **1976**; Fundamental Duties are **non-justiciable**. ⚠️ Non-justiciability is a legal fact and settles no moral thesis.

### F. Accountability — three dimensions, three arenas, one asymmetry

| Dimension | Demand | Failure if isolated |
|---|---|---|
| **Answerability** ✅ | information plus public justification | ritual |
| **Enforceability** ✅ | review, remedy, sanction, removal | arbitrariness |
| **Responsiveness** ⚠️ | changed conduct, repaired harm, institutional learning | correction without prospective rules |

| Arena | To whom | Through what |
|---|---|---|
| **Political** ✅ | the people | representation, elections, opposition, deliberation, publicity, criticism |
| **Legal** ✅ | law and courts | grievance, restraint of arbitrariness, redress |
| **Moral** ✅ | conscience, civil society, history | criticism, public shame, historical judgment |

✅ **The asymmetry to write:** a legally wrongful act does **not** become right because it is electorally supported. ⚠️ **Two negations:** accountability is not efficiency, and it does not contradict finality — it governs the exercise of final authority.

### G. Dworkin — the rights-to-accountability bridge

| | Rules | Principles |
|---|---|---|
| Application ✅ | all-or-nothing | weight; can be outweighed and survive |
| Conflict ✅ | one must be invalid or excepted | genuinely compete and are balanced |
| Authority ✅ | pedigree | moral-political argument justifying the practice |
| Judicial role ✅ | apply | interpret and weigh |

✅ **Illustration:** no one may profit from his own wrong — the murderer is denied the inheritance a valid will conferred; the principle overrides the rule **without invalidating it**. ✅ **Upshot:** rights are **trumps** against collective goals, so adjudication is accountability. ❓ **Residual:** the right-answer thesis is hard to sustain under permanent reasonable disagreement. ⚠️ **Boundary:** Austin → Kelsen → Hart belongs to **Sovereignty**; name and route.

### H. Political obligation — the four tests and the six families

**Tests ⚠️:** generality · particularity · content-independence · defeasibility.

| Family | Core claim | Anchor | Failure |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Consent / contract** ✅ | voluntary undertaking, express or tacit | Locke; Hobbes; Rousseau | express consent almost never given; tacit consent not voluntary |
| **Fair play** ✅ | accept benefits, owe burdens | Hart; Rawls | public order is non-excludable; unsolicited benefit creates no debt |
| **Natural duty of justice** ✅ | support just institutions applying to us | Rawls | fails **particularity** |
| **Associative** ✅ | constitutive identities and roles | Dworkin; communitarians | membership however unjust |
| **Gratitude** ✅ | benefactor supplies conditions of decent life | classical and Indian duty frameworks | some reciprocity, not general obedience |
| **Anti-obligation** ✅ | no general obligation demonstrated | Wolff; A. John Simmons | cannot distinguish legitimate state from private agency |

❌ **Attribution control:** **Simmons** is the sceptic; **Raz** supplies the **service conception** — authority legitimate when its directives improve conformity with reasons that already apply, functioning as **pre-emptive** reasons. Never swap them.

### I. The disobedience ladder — four rungs on five criteria

| Form | Aim | Publicity | Legality | Scope | System |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Conscientious objection** ✅ | avoid complicity | may be private | seeks exemption; accepts penalty | own act | accepts legitimacy |
| **Civil disobedience** ✅ | persuade the majority | essentially public | breach with acceptance of penalty | a specific law | accepts legitimacy |
| **Resistance** ✅ | obstruct a grave injustice | may be covert | may refuse penalty | particular acts | denies those acts' legitimacy |
| **Revolution** ✅ | replace the order | organised, often armed | rejects the order | whole system | denies legitimacy |

⚠️ **Two defining features of civil disobedience:** publicity with appeal to shared principles, and **acceptance of the legal penalty**. Non-violence is the standard third condition; on the Gandhian account it is **constitutive**.

**Dated anchors ✅:** Thoreau, *Civil Disobedience*, **1849** (first published as "Resistance to Civil Government") — individual conscientious refusal, **not** an organised campaign. Gandhi, Salt Satyagraha — march begun **March 1930**, salt law broken **6 April 1930**; *satyāgraha* is open to **self-suffering**. Ambedkar, Constituent Assembly concluding address, **25 November 1949** — the **"grammar of anarchy"**. ⚠️ Gandhi and Ambedkar disagree about the **availability condition**, not about the concept.

### J. India — precise, and always with the caution

- ✅ **Articles 19(1)(a) and 19(1)(b):** freedom of speech and expression; right to assemble **peaceably and without arms**; **subject to reasonable restrictions** under Articles 19(2) and 19(3). They protect **protest, not law-breaking**.
- ✅ **Article 51A:** Fundamental Duties, inserted by the Constitution (Forty-second Amendment) Act, **1976**; **non-justiciable**.
- ⚠️ **Controlling caution:** no provision, statute or judgment settles the philosophical question. **Legal permission is not moral justification; legal prohibition is not moral refutation.**
- ❌ Never claim any Indian statute or judgment "recognises a right to civil disobedience".

### K. Trap list — final pre-submission check

1. ❌ Bentham rejected **all** rights. ✅ He rejects **natural** rights, not legal rights as such.
2. ❌ Hohfeld reduced to one formula. ✅ Four incidents; only the claim-right is strictly correlative to a duty.
3. ❌ Correlativity as simple logical identity everywhere. ⚠️ Strongest as a normative-juridical thesis; false for liberties.
4. ❌ Gandhi portrayed as anti-rights. ✅ He reorders the moral source of rights through duty.
5. ❌ Accountability collapsed into elections. ✅ Political, legal and moral; three dimensions, not one mechanism.
6. ❌ Indian tradition "simply antagonistic" to rights. ⚠️ A duty-centred idiom that both enriches and complicates rights discourse.
7. ❌ Limitation equated with destruction of rights. ✅ Principled limits are the condition of coexistence.
8. ❌ Hobbes, Locke and Rousseau treated as saying the same thing. ✅ Freedom, authority and rights differ sharply across the three.
9. ❌ Mechanistic and organic read as literal science. ✅ Rival logical metaphors about explanatory priority.
10. ❌ Liberalism flattened into atomism. ⚠️ Welfare liberalism and neutrality recognise social formation and public goods.
11. ❌ Hegel as defender of every actual state. ⚠️ The claim concerns rational ethical institutions.
12. ❌ Positive freedom equated with unlimited paternalism. ✅ Enabling provision stays rights-bound; the harm principle disciplines coercion for a person's own good.
13. ❌ The private sphere called non-political in every respect. ⚠️ Privacy protects autonomy but cannot immunise harm or domination.
14. ❌ Aristotle called a modern individualist. ✅ A comparative middle only; both are perfectionists and his exclusions stand.
15. ❌ The four rungs of disobedience merged. ✅ They differ in aim, publicity, legality, scope and attitude to the system.
16. ❌ Simmons and Raz swapped. ✅ Simmons the sceptic; Raz the service conception.
17. ❌ Article 51A mis-dated or treated as moral proof. ✅ Forty-second Amendment, 1976; non-justiciable; settles nothing moral.
18. ❌ Thoreau presented as Gandhi's blueprint. ⚠️ Individual withdrawal against organised penalty-accepting campaign.

### L. PYQ ownership (2018–2025): exactly 9 owner parts, and the route for each

| Year | Part | Marks | Demand | Route |
|---|---|---:|---|---|
| 2018 | Q4(a) | 20 | human rights and dignity as a common human aspiration | S7 → aspiration against articulation; three-column grid; minimum core |
| 2019 | Q2(a) | 20 | do rights make citizens accountable to the State, in the Indian context | S13–S14 → three dimensions, three arenas, Dworkin, reciprocal verdict |
| 2020 | Q2(c) | 15 | is Indian tradition antagonistic to individual rights | S8 → fix the test; *dharma* idiom; Gandhi bridge; Ambedkar concession |
| 2021 | Q4(c) | 15 | does social contract adequately address human rights | S18 → name the benchmark; Locke and Rousseau; three excluded categories |
| 2022 | Q4(b) | 15 | do unconditional rights necessarily lead to anarchy | S9 → define both words; collision; four boundary principles; accountability |
| 2023 | Q2(b) | 15 | duty and accountability prior to rights | S12 → criterion first; three-position grid; accountability as the safeguard |
| 2024 | Q1(b) | 10 | origin and development of social contract theory | S18 → transfer, trust, general will as **changes** |
| 2024 | Q3(a) | 20 | Aristotle more successful than Plato between statism and individualism | S19 → the intermediate sphere; five axes; perfectionism and exclusions |
| 2025 | Q4(c) | 15 | duties as obligation, rights as entitlement, no necessary connection | S5 + S12 → concede premise, refuse inference, Hohfeldian split |

**Directive decoder ⚠️** — **Explain / What is meant by:** definition → components → one distinction → one example. **Discuss:** doctrine → rival → objection → reply → verdict. **Critically examine:** two objections, each with reply and **residual**. **Are X and Y necessarily connected?** attack the modal word; distinguish conceptual necessity from institutional desirability. **Should X have priority over Y?** name the criterion, then rank, then concede what the loser secures. **Does X adequately address Y?** fix the benchmark, test, identify the specific inadequacy. **Trace origin and development:** chronology **plus** what each thinker changes.

**Answer methods ⚠️** — **10 marks (~150–220 words, 4 moves, ~12 minutes):** exact concept in technical vocabulary → analytic components → one evidence unit with its limitation → graded verdict. ❌ Do not run all six obligation families or all four rungs at ten marks. **15 marks (~250–330 words, 6 moves, ~18 minutes):** frame the axis of dispute → position A with its presupposition → strongest rival → one table → one worked objection → reply → residual → conditional verdict. **20 marks (~330–400 words, 8 moves, ~25 minutes):** provisional thesis using the stem's verb → conceptual map and criterion → strongest case as premises → strongest rival tradition → two objection–reply chains → one structure deployed as the spine → one dated Indian illustration with the caution → graded verdict conceding something to the losing side.

**Graded verdict formulas ⚠️ (adapt; never reproduce mechanically)** — *Modal:* "Claim-rights are necessarily correlative to duties; liberties are not — so the connection is conceptual for one class of rights and merely institutional for another." *Conditional:* "The obligation holds where institutions are substantially just and channels genuinely open, and lapses where they are not; the question is one of degree, not of kind." *Narrowed-scepticism:* "The sceptic defeats the claim to a general, content-independent duty of obedience, but not the claim that most particular laws are independently binding." *Ladder:* "Conscientious refusal, civil disobedience, resistance and revolution answer to progressively heavier burdens of justification; the stem is answered by locating the case on that ladder." *Reconciliation:* "A mature polity does not choose between rights and duties; it constitutionalises rights, cultivates duties and institutionalises accountability, since each without the others is unstable." *Dignity–authority:* "The individual is neither an atom outside society nor an expendable part of the state; the task is to specify a justified relation between dignity and authority."

**Link-outs** ✅ — [Sovereignty] for limits on supreme power and the Austin–Kelsen–Hart sequence; [Political Ideologies] for anarchism, Marxism and socialism as ideologies; [Social and Political Ideals] for equality, justice and liberty themselves; [Forms of Government] for monarchy, theocracy and democracy; [Humanism, Secularism, Multiculturalism] for dignity and universalism debates; [Caste, Gandhi, Ambedkar] for the caste-specific reading of duty and constitutional rights.

**Sources** ✅ — O. P. Gauba, *An Introduction to Political Theory*, searchable local PDF pp. 220–300 and 338–390, supplying the organic, liberal-individualist, welfare, class, communitarian, Gandhian and pluralist taxonomy at pp. 220–279; *Socio-Political Philosophy*, local compiled notes PDF pp. 68–83 and 129–132, no named author asserted; Rajeev Bhargava and Ashok Acharya on political theory and Indian political thought; Harold J. Laski, *A Grammar of Politics*; John Locke, *Two Treatises of Government*; Thomas Hobbes, *Leviathan*; Jean-Jacques Rousseau, *The Social Contract*; Wesley Newcomb Hohfeld, *Fundamental Legal Conceptions*; Ronald Dworkin, *Taking Rights Seriously* (**1977**); A. John Simmons on political obligation; Joseph Raz on authority and the service conception; H. L. A. Hart and John Rawls on fair play and the natural duty of justice; Henry David Thoreau, *Civil Disobedience* (**1849**); M. K. Gandhi on *satyāgraha*, with the Salt Satyagraha of **1930** as a dated illustration; B. R. Ambedkar, concluding address to the Constituent Assembly, **25 November 1949**; the Constitution of India, Articles 19 and 51A, used as dated constitutional illustration only; Plato, *Republic*; Aristotle, *Politics* Books I–II. ⚠️ The political-obligation grid, the Dworkin module and the disobedience ladder are named-scholar reconstructions: no page, chapter, edition or verbatim wording is asserted, no empirical claim is made about any Indian government, party or period, and the Austin → Kelsen → Hart sequence is deliberately not developed here.

THE QUESTION EACH ASKS
  WILL     -> WHO CONTROLS the duty?
  INTEREST -> WHOSE IMPORTANT INTEREST JUSTIFIES imposing the duty?
        |
        v
THE MIRRORED HARD CASES -- EACH BREAKS ON THE OTHER'S HOME GROUND
  INFANT / SEVERELY DISABLED -> strict WILL theory implies NO RIGHTS (intolerable);
                                INTEREST theory handles easily
  COMPETENT ADULT WHO WAIVES -> WILL theory handles easily (waiver IS exercise);
                                strict INTEREST theory struggles to explain discarding a
                                protection that exists for the holder's benefit
        |
        v
THE MIXED POSITION -- A CONCLUSION, NOT A DODGE
  rights BOTH secure AGENCY and protect VITAL INTERESTS; different rights sit at different
  points on that spectrum, and a legal order need not pick one theory for all of them
OBJECTION -> the theories cannot be reconciled. REPLY -> mature systems ALREADY use both
  dimensions. RESIDUAL -> when agency and interest CONFLICT (paternalist protection against
  competent refusal), the mixed view still owes a TIE-BREAKING CRITERION, and has none.

THE 2018 STEM SUPPLIES ITS OWN THESIS

WHAT MUST NOT BE CONCEDED
  that the critique of CRUELTY, ARBITRARY POWER and DEGRADATION is itself merely LOCAL.
  If that goes, "human rights" names NOTHING.
OBJECTION -> universalism erases civilisational diversity. REPLY -> universality need NOT
  mean cultural HOMOGENISATION. RESIDUAL -> the BOUNDARY of the minimum core, and WHO
  fixes it, remain genuinely disputed.

ASCII MASTER FLOW — PANEL 8/25: Indian tradition, Gandhi, and the 2020 test
THE STEM CONTAINS TWO INSTRUCTIONS
  (i) decide ANTAGONISM -- a strong word that must be TESTED
  (ii) do it THROUGH the HUMAN-RIGHTS DOCTRINE, not by general cultural comparison
        |
        v
THREE POSSIBLE ANSWERS -- ONLY ONE SURVIVES
  "YES, ANTAGONISTIC" -> would require the tradition to DEFEND CRUELTY or DENY MORAL
                         WORTH. It does not. FAILS.
  "NO, IDENTICAL" ----> would require CLAIM-RIGHTS ASSERTED AGAINST THE STATE. Not there.
                         FAILS.
  "DIFFERENT IDIOM, NOT ANTAGONISM" + a NAMED RISK -> SURVIVES.
        |
        v
THE IDIOM, STATED PRECISELY
  DHARMA -> role-based obligation, reciprocity, self-restraint, truth and order
  THE PERSON -> EMBEDDED in a web of relations, not an abstract individual outside all ties
  EMPHASIS -> RIGHT CONDUCT, SELF-MASTERY, RECIPROCAL OBLIGATION
  AT ITS STRONGEST -> RESPONSIBILITY, RESTRAINT, MORAL VOCATION -- NOT mere obedience
  REQUIRED FILTER -> must NOT sacralise HIERARCHY
        |
        v
GANDHI'S BRIDGE
  "rights grow out of WELL-PERFORMED DUTIES"
  -> a thesis about the MORAL SOURCE and SOCIAL SUSTAINABILITY of rights, NOT their denial
  -> DUTY-FIRST is NOT necessarily HIERARCHY-FIRST
  -> SWARAJ = SELF-RULE in the ETHICAL sense, not merely external non-interference
  -> Gandhi is NEITHER a hard legal positivist NOR an atomistic liberal; NEITHER simply
     anti-state NOR a centralising statist -- MORALISED CITIZENSHIP and DECENTRALISED
     RESPONSIBILITY
        |
        v
THE HONEST CONCESSION
  a duty-centred idiom can leave LITTLE ROOM FOR PROTEST and can SUBMERGE the person
  AMBEDKAR -> in a society STILL STRATIFIED BY CASTE, duty-first framings can be CAPTURED
              by dominant groups
  VERDICT -> ANTECEDENT to the rights idiom, not ANTAGONISTIC; combine duty-language with
             CONSTITUTIONAL RIGHTS and MORAL EQUALITY -- a filtered synthesis, not nostalgia

ASCII MASTER FLOW — PANEL 9/25: Unconditional rights and the collision argument (2022)
TWO WORDS DO ALL THE WORK -- ATTACK BOTH
  "UNCONDITIONAL" = admitting NO LIMIT WHATEVER, not merely "strong"
  "NECESSARILY"   = a MODAL claim about CONCEPTUAL necessity, not a social forecast
        |
        v
THE COLLISION
  MY UNLIMITED LIBERTY <------------> YOUR EQUAL UNLIMITED LIBERTY
  both claims cannot be satisfied, and NO PROCEDURE can decide between them --
  because ANY resolving procedure would ITSELF BE A CONDITION
  HOHFELDIAN REFINEMENT -> two unconditional LIBERTIES conflict IN FACT; two unconditional
  CLAIM-RIGHTS conflict IN NORMATIVE STRUCTURE, since each entails a duty in the other
  party that the other's right forbids
        |
        v
THE FOUR BOUNDARY PRINCIPLES THAT RESTORE COEXISTENCE
  1. OTHERS' EQUAL RIGHTS   2. HARM CONSTRAINTS
  3. PUBLIC ORDER           4. INSTITUTIONAL ADJUDICATION
  -> not DENIAL of rights but the CONDITION OF THEIR COEXISTENCE, since liberty is PLURAL
     and SOCIALLY SITUATED
        |
        v
THE DECISIVE DISTINCTION
  LIMITING a right IS NOT NULLIFYING it
    LIMITATION ..... principled, reasoned, bounded, REVIEWABLE
    NULLIFICATION .. arbitrary, unreasoned, open-ended, UNREVIEWABLE
  THE PROBLEM IS *ARBITRARY RESTRICTION*, NOT PRINCIPLED COORDINATION
        |
        v
OBJECTION -> once limits are admitted, the state may STEADILY ERODE rights
REPLY -> close on ACCOUNTABILITY: every restriction JUSTIFIED, PROPORTIONATE and
  JUDICIALLY REVIEWABLE -- the limits must themselves be LIMITED
RESIDUAL -> where review is CAPTURED, DELAYED or UNAVAILABLE to the affected group, the
  erosion objection returns in full; that vulnerability is EMPIRICAL and undischarged
        |
        v
VERDICT -> unconditional rights are SELF-DEFEATING rather than NECESSARILY
  ANARCHY-PRODUCING: they destroy the COEXISTENCE that makes rights exercisable, so the
  failure is INTERNAL TO THE CONCEPT

ASCII MASTER FLOW — PANEL 10/25: Duties -- nature, kinds, and what law cannot codify
DEFINITION -> an OBLIGATION to DO, AVOID, RESPECT or SUPPORT something owed to OTHERS,
              INSTITUTIONS, COMMUNITY or ONESELF
WHAT DUTIES DO -> turn ABSTRACT NORMS into EXPECTATIONS OF CONDUCT, so that freedom can be
              exercised in a SHARED world
PRESUPPOSITION -> human beings are RELATIONAL; social life cannot be sustained by CLAIMS
              ALONE
KINDS -> LEGAL - MORAL - CIVIC - POLITICAL - PROFESSIONAL - SPIRITUAL
         X do NOT reduce duty to LEGAL duty
        |
        v
TYPE          BASIS                          ENFORCEMENT              EXAMPLE-TYPE
------------  -----------------------------  -----------------------  --------------------
LEGAL DUTY    law, constitution, statute,    courts, penalties,       obeying valid law;
              recognised procedure           official sanction        paying due taxes
MORAL DUTY    conscience, ethical reason,    praise and blame,        truthfulness;
              social norm                    self-judgment, public    compassion; civic
                                             opinion                  decency
        |
        v
THE CONTROLLING ARGUMENT
  a legal order CANNOT FUNCTION WITHOUT COMPLIANCE -- but NO LEGAL ORDER CAN CODIFY THE
  WHOLE OF ETHICAL LIFE. Democratic citizenship depends on habits LAW CANNOT MANUFACTURE:
  TOLERANCE - HONESTY - RESTRAINT - RESPECT FOR DISAGREEMENT - CONCERN FOR PUBLIC GOOD
TRAFFIC BETWEEN THE COLUMNS -> some duties BEGIN moral and BECOME legal; others SHOULD
  REMAIN moral, because coercion would be SELF-DEFEATING
        |
        v
THE STANDING DANGER, NAMED HONESTLY
  duty is often invoked BY AUTHORITY TO SILENCE DISSENT
  REPLY -> tie duty to JUSTICE, RECIPROCITY and ACCOUNTABILITY; the test is DIRECTIONAL --
           does the duty-talk BIND THE POWERFUL TOO?
  RESIDUAL -> even reciprocal duty can be captured, because the TERMS of reciprocity are
           set by whoever controls the institutions

ASCII MASTER FLOW — PANEL 11/25: Gandhi's inversion and the Fundamental Duties
THE LIBERAL ORDER              GANDHI'S ORDER
I HAVE rights                  I PERFORM duties
   -> others owe me                -> rights become SECURE and SUSTAINABLE
   -> the state SECURES what        -> the moral community GROWS the conditions of right
      I already have
        |
        v
WHAT GANDHI IS *NOT* SAYING -- ALL FOUR MUST BE STATED
  X rights are dispensable      X he is a hard legal positivist
  X he is an atomistic liberal  X he is a centralising statist
  YES -> DUTY EDUCATES THE MORAL SELF, so that freedom becomes NON-DESTRUCTIVE
  KEY TERM -> SWARAJ = ethical SELF-RULE, not merely external non-interference
        |
        v
THE CONSTITUTIONAL REGISTER -- ARTICLE 51A
  INSERTED BY -> the Constitution (FORTY-SECOND AMENDMENT) Act, 1976
  PHILOSOPHICAL READING -> an attempt to BALANCE rights-bearing citizenship with
    RESPONSIBILITY-bearing citizenship; a republic cannot survive if citizens invoke rights
    while neglecting the CIVIC ETHOS that preserves institutions, social harmony, public
    property, scientific temper and constitutional culture
  LEGAL FACT -> Fundamental Duties are NON-JUSTICIABLE -- not directly enforceable
  PHILOSOPHICAL CONTROL -> NON-JUSTICIABILITY IS A LEGAL FACT; it NEITHER PROVES NOR
    DISPROVES any MORAL thesis about duty
  X Fundamental Duties are NOT a warrant to DIMINISH rights
        |
        v
OBJECTION -> duty-language is STATE-SPONSORED MORALISM
REPLY -> it becomes problematic EXACTLY when deployed to EVADE STATE ACCOUNTABILITY or to
  SUPPRESS LEGITIMATE DISSENT; citizenship is PARTICIPATORY, not CONSUMPTIVE
RESIDUAL -> AMBEDKAR: a duty-first order in a stratified society can be CAPTURED by
  dominant groups, so the method that liberates one group may ENTRENCH another

2025 IS A *MODAL* QUESTION            2023 IS A *PRIORITY* QUESTION
"no NECESSARY connection"             "duty and accountability PRIORITY over rights?"
  -> attack "NECESSARILY"               -> NAME A CRITERION BEFORE RANKING

                 to a DUTY             ------------  ---------------  ---------  ----------
  LIBERTY ----> NO correlative duty    RIGHTS-FIRST  protects person  shields    atomised
                 to assist                           against          minorities entitlement
  POWER ------> LIABILITY                            coercion         + dissent  culture

        |                                            and reciprocity  social     or
        v                                                             responsi-  conformity
THE PREMISE IS TRUE, THE INFERENCE                                    bility
IS INVALID -- SAY BOTH                 BALANCED      mutually         best fit   requires
  TRUE ..... duties ARE obligations                  sustaining       for        institu-
             and rights ARE                                           constitu-  tional
             entitlements: ANALYTICALLY                               tional     vigilance
             DISTINGUISHABLE                                          democracy
  INVALID .. distinguishable does NOT
             entail UNCONNECTED --
             they are NOT SOCIALLY
